1.1.3 Modeling Systems with Built-in Automatic Differentiation 

ExaModels.jl targets NLPs of the structured form discussed above and supplies its own AD, so what follows focuses on the AMSs that operate in the same space. These are the systems that target classical mathematical programming problems, including general smooth NLPs, and that ship with their own AD machinery. Five such systems are reviewed below, and Table 1 compares them with ExaModels.jl. AMPL (Fourer, Gay, and Kernighan, 1990) is a standalone language with its own parser, exposing the NLP function callbacks through the ASL via the .nl interchange format; GAMS (Bussieck and Meeraus, 2004) emerged around the same time and is similar in design. JuMP (Dunning, Huchette, and Lubin, 2017; Lubin, Dowson, et al., 2023) is embedded in Julia; through macros and operator overloading it builds a per-scalar expression graph and runs reverse-mode AD on the graph. CasADi (Andersson et al., 2019) is a C++ symbolic framework whose central abstraction is the Function , a compiled expression graph that can be reused, embedded, or emitted as standalone C code; it supports both forward and reverse AD, and uses graph coloring to evaluate sparse Jacobians and Hessians. Gravity (Hijazi, Wang, and Coffrin, 2018) introduces “constraint templates” to preserve the pattern structure of indexed constraints. We compare these systems along two axes: how the partially separable, repetitive structure of indexed constraints is preserved and exploited, and whether the NLP functions are evaluated by compiled or interpreted code. The first axis is structure exploitation, and most systems do not exploit the repetitive structure. AMPL, GAMS, and JuMP build flat, per-scalar expression graphs at model construction, where every constraint instance becomes a node in a single large graph that AD traverses at evaluation time. JuMP is the canonical example of iterator flattening. Indexed-constraint syntax such as @constraint(model, [i=1:N], ...) is lowered to a flat scalar collection before AD, so the iteration visible to the user is no longer visible to the AD machinery. Two strategies preserve indexed-constraint structure at the modeling layer: vectorization and templatizing. Vectorization expresses an indexed family as a single matrix-algebra operation, so the repetition lives in the dimensions of the matrices and vectors rather than in enumerated scalar expressions. Templatizing declares a single algebraic expression parameterized by a data set and evaluates that one expression across all instances, so the repetition lives in the data rather than in the expression. The two strategies differ in abstraction rather than reach. Heterogeneous indexed structure, meaning general repeated expressions whose size or form varies per index, such as variable-size sums, can be expressed in either form. Vectorization shifts the work to pre-constructing sparse incidence matrices, while templatizing expresses the repeated expression directly. The representative implementation of vectorization is CasADi, which compiles each Function once and reuses it across calls. CasADi supports structure preservation through vectorized expressions of its matrix symbolic type, MX. The slice x[1:] - x[:-1] stays a single matrix-level node, and both the symbolic graph and the generated C function body remain constant-size as the discretization grows; only the loop bound and workspace offsets change. This requires the model to be written as matrix algebra. Heterogeneous indexed structure, such as ACOPF power balance over each bus’s generators and incident lines, can be encoded via sparse incidence matrices and sparse matrix–vector products. The result is mathematically equivalent to a templatized model, but uses a different abstraction: matrix algebra over pre-constructed sparsity patterns 

6versus per-index expressions over data sets. Machine learning frameworks such as JAX (Bradbury et al., 2018) and PyTorch (Paszke et al., 2019) are not widely used for formulating and solving classical mathematical programs, but they support the same matrix algebra syntax and admit natural vectorization. The model is written in whole-array operations and differentiated by the framework’s AD. They are nonetheless natural companions for GPU evaluation of the NLP functions: gradient evaluation is directly supported, and the sparse Jacobian and Hessian can be evaluated through coloring-based sparse AD. Templatizing as an evaluation strategy is a more recent idea, first explored in Gravity through its constraint templates: a constraint is declared once over an index set, and the repeated structure is exploited to reduce the evaluation time of the NLP functions, including the Jacobian and the Hessian. Gravity is implemented as a C++ library in which the numerical accuracy of variables and parameters is specifiable by the user. It further offers convexity detection, multithreading of subproblems, and lazy constraint generation, and the authors report function evaluation five times faster and memory use up to 60 times lower than JuMP (Hijazi, Wang, and Coffrin, 2018). The indexed declaration syntax itself is common: JuMP’s @constraint(model, [i=1:N], ...) and Pyomo’s Constraint(index set, rule=f) both declare an indexed constraint family in a single statement. To the best of our knowledge, however, neither system specializes the evaluation internally to exploit the structure this syntax exposes, and the declaration is unrolled into flat per-scalar instances. Because this indexed style has long been standard in the AMSs of mathematical programming and operations research, such as AMPL, GAMS, JuMP, and Pyomo, the modeling syntax of ExaModels.jl remains close to that of existing modeling tools. ExaModels.jl follows the templatizing strategy but goes beyond what Gravity has proposed. It provides a general abstraction that can represent diverse repetitive patterns, together with an implementation built on it, namely type-stable parameterized expression trees compiled per pattern by the Julia compiler (Section 4). This design naturally leads to GPU parallelization, which we implement through KernelAbstractions.jl and demonstrate in this paper (Section 6). As of this writing, neither CasADi nor Gravity supports GPU-native derivative evaluation. The second axis is the evaluation path, compiled versus interpreted, where compilation reduces per-callback runtime overhead at the cost of longer build time. AMPL parses models to the .nl format, which is evaluated by ASL with compiled operator implementations; a legacy nlc tool can emit standalone C. GAMS uses a similar built-in pipeline. CasADi provides both options: by default a Function is evaluated through CasADi’s runtime, which walks a flat operation sequence with compiled operator implementations; alternatively, standalone C code can be emitted via codegen. Gravity uses symbolic differentiation and dispatches compiled C++ templates. JuMP is embedded in the JIT-compiled Julia language, but its default AD does not compile a specialized derivative kernel per constraint type. It is a general-purpose interpreter that walks the expression graph at each callback, so it cannot be regarded as a compiled AD approach. The exception is its SymbolicAD backend (Dowson and Schanen, 2022), which reduces each constraint to a canonical symbolic form and computes a single symbolic derivative per distinct constraint structure. The resulting compiled evaluator is then reused across all instances that share that structure. ExaModels.jl is firmly in the compiled category, and it compiles aggressively. It builds a parameterized expression tree at model-construction time and applies reverse-mode AD directly to that tree (Section 5). Because the tree is type-stable end-to-end, each callback compiles into a single chunk of LLVM code specialized to the problem’s algebraic structure (Section 7.2.1). This gives the Julia compiler full opportunity to optimize the NLP functions, and it also makes the callbacks straightforward to convert into GPU kernels (Section 6). 

## 1.2 Contributions 

The contributions of this paper are methodological (M), implementational (I), and experimental (E). 

M1. We introduce a SIMD abstraction for NLPs that represents a problem as a small number of algebraic patterns, each evaluated over many data points. The abstraction preserves the pattern–data separation throughout model construction and reduces NLP function evaluation to embarrassingly parallel loops over data points, which is the structure that GPU execution requires. The closest prior idea is Gravity’s constraint templates (Hijazi, Wang, and Coffrin, 2018); our abstraction goes beyond theirs by providing 

7a general representation that covers diverse repetitive patterns and extends naturally to parallel GPU evaluation. 

M2. We develop a coloring-free sparse AD algorithm that exploits regular per-pattern sparsity to compute Jacobians and Hessians in a partially compressed COO form. Unlike the coloring-based sparse AD of standard practice (Gebremedhin, Manne, and Pothen, 2005), the sparsity analysis is applied at the level of the parameterized expression tree, and the derivative values are partially compressed over each tree. This minimizes the fill-in while eliminating the race conditions that arise when the values are accumulated in parallel, so that element-wise, coloring-free sparse AD can be implemented for GPUs. To the best of our knowledge, this approach is new. 

I1. We present, to our knowledge, the first AMS with cross-platform GPU-native derivative evaluation, portable across NVIDIA, AMD, Intel, and Apple devices via KernelAbstractions.jl (Churavy, 2025); the same model code runs on all backends without modification. 

I2. We support AOT compilation to standalone binaries independent of the Julia runtime, with no code generation step. Whereas systems such as CasADi and CVXPY provide separate code-generation tools for deployment, in ExaModels.jl exactly the same code serves both JIT and AOT compilation; to our knowledge, it is the first AMS with this property. 

I3. We support arbitrary numeric precision by exploiting the type flexibility of the Julia language. Without modification, the same model code runs in Float64 ; in Float32 , including on hardware that supports only single precision; and in user-defined types realizing software-emulated extended precision, such as the double-double arithmetic of DoubleFloats.jl (Sarnoff and JuliaMath, 2022). Existing AMSs are typically fixed to double precision. Among those reviewed above, Gravity allows variables and parameters to be declared over a fixed set of built-in C++ numeric types (float, double, long double, integer, and complex), though not over user-defined types, and JuMP’s nonlinear interface is restricted to Float64 .

E1. We contribute ExaModels-native implementations of the Lukˇ san–Vlˇ cek (Lukˇ san and Vlˇ cek, 1999), COPS (Dolan and Mor´ e, 2001), and PGLIB-OPF benchmark libraries. We systematically measure derivative evaluation performance on the CPU and across multiple GPU backends. These benchmark implementations are a contribution in their own right: they run natively on the GPU and can be reused in later studies for the performance benchmarking of GPU-resident NLP algorithms. We report GPU speedups of up to 76 × over single-threaded CPU evaluation. 

Relationship to prior work. The concept of the SIMD abstraction first appeared in Shin, Anitescu, and Pacaud (2024) as part of an ACOPF-specific GPU optimization framework. The abstraction itself, the parameterized expression tree, the coloring-free sparse AD algorithm, and the multi-backend GPU implementation have not previously been elaborated, and this is the role of the present paper. Several subsequent works use ExaModels.jl as the modeling layer: Johnson et al. (2025) provides a power-systems library on top of it; the GPU-native condensed-space interior-point methods of Pacaud, Shin, et al. (2024) consume its callbacks; and Pacaud and Shin (2024) reports a fully GPU-resident optimization pipeline pairing ExaModels.jl with MadNLP.jl (Shin, Pacaud, and Montoison, 2025). 

## 1.3 Limitations 

The proposed approach also carries drawbacks that follow directly from its design choices. 

• The approach is runtime-optimized at the cost of a compilation overhead: the NLP function callbacks are compiled specifically for each algebraic pattern, so every new expression structure must be compiled at least once before it can be evaluated. This places a burden on the compiler and adds a one-time compilation latency ahead of the first evaluation. The design favors runtime performance over the ability to quickly try out many formulations, which may not be ideal for rapid prototyping across structurally different models. It is best suited, for example, to a mature model deployed for fast repeated evaluation, as in real-time decision making. 

8• The user is required to write the model in the SIMD-compatible form. It is easier in many cases to state a model through explicit loops over scalar constraints. The pattern–data separation also requires, in some cases, the data arrays to be created before the objective and constraint patterns can be declared, which may require restructuring compared with the natural formulation in other modeling systems. 

• The benefits are contingent on the problem being partially separable and repetitive, and a model with many distinct patterns and few data points per pattern gains little. Components that cannot be expressed as parameterized expression trees, such as constraints with data-dependent branching or embedded simulation codes, fall outside the abstraction. These are instead accommodated through external oracles (Section 7.5), which do not carry the performance guarantees of the abstraction. 

• The templatized syntax expresses the NLP functions entirely through scalar operations. Unless the external oracle mechanism is used, the evaluation invokes no vectorized library routines, such as BLAS kernels or sparse matrix–vector products, and therefore cannot benefit from such optimized libraries. 

## 1.4 Outline 

The remainder of this paper falls into three parts, with the fundamentals developed in a top-down manner. Section 2 presents the modeling abstraction and the user- and solver-facing interfaces. Section 3 presents the data structures behind them: the model containers and the internal types that represent variables, parameters, objectives, and constraints, whose common building block is the SIMDFunction . Section 4 presents the parameterized expression tree, the core idea within the SIMDFunction , and describes how individual nodes and leaves are put together to form the tree. Section 5 explains how this tree is traversed to evaluate the derivatives with the coloring-free sparse AD algorithm. These fundamentals are then realized in a high-performance, portable implementation. Section 6 describes the GPU acceleration via KernelAbstractions.jl, and Section 7 collects implementation-level details deferred from the main development, including the extension of the abstraction to two-stage stochastic and batch problems. Finally, the experiments are reported in Section 8, and Section 9 concludes the paper. 

## 1.5 Notation 

We use Rn for n-dimensional real space. Code identifiers are typeset in monospace . We further distinguish a mathematical quantity from its software representation: for example, x denotes a vector as a mathematical object, whereas x refers to the array that stores it. We also use a few standard Julia conventions throughout. Indexing is one-based, following Julia’s convention. Variable blocks may nonetheless be declared over arbitrary integer index ranges, such as the 0:nh blocks of the Goddard rocket model in Section 2. The symbol <: 

denotes the subtype relation, so Node1 <: AbstractNode reads “ Node1 is a subtype of AbstractNode .” A trailing ! marks a function that mutates its arguments in place (e.g., set value! ). A leading @ marks a macro, one of Julia’s metaprogramming facilities. Rather than being called at run time, a macro receives its arguments as unevaluated syntax and rewrites them, at parse time, into an equivalent block of ordinary code that is then compiled in its place. Writing @add var , for instance, is shorthand for the expanded sequence of statements the macro emits. A name written with curly braces, Type {I, J, ... }, is a parametric type :the entries I, J, . . . are its type parameters, on which the compiler specializes the generated code (e.g., 

DataIndexed {I, J }). An anonymous function is written (args) -> body (e.g., (i, x) -> x[i+1]^2 ). 

# 2 Modeling Abstraction and Interfaces 

ExaModels.jl sits between the user and the solver (Figure 1). The user states a problem in mathematical terms, while the solver asks for numerical values of the NLP functions at each iteration. An AMS must therefore expose an interface on both sides: a solver-facing callback interface for evaluating the model and a user-facing syntax for building it. On the solver side, the interface is largely standard. ExaModels.jl follows the convention of NLPMod-els.jl (Orban, Soares Siqueira, and contributors, 2023), which fixes the model–solver interface as a set of meta-information accessors and callback functions for objective, constraint, gradient, Jacobian, and Hessian 

9User       

> @add var ,@add par ,
> @add obj ,@add expr ,
> @add con ,@add con! ,
> set parameter! ,
> solution ,multipliers
> ExaModels.jl
> stores the model in the SIMD form (3); exposes meta-information and callbacks for the NLP (2)
> Solver
> obj ,grad! ,cons! ,
> jac coord! ,
> hess coord!
> algebraic model structured solution meta-information and callbacks flat solution

Figure 1: ExaModels.jl between the user and the solver. Solid arrows carry the model forward from the modeling syntax to the solver callbacks; dashed arrows carry the solution back. evaluation. An ExaModels.jl model can therefore be passed directly to solvers for general NLPs, such as Ipopt (W¨ achter and Biegler, 2006), Uno (Vanaret and Leyffer, 2026), and MadNLP.jl (Shin, Pacaud, and Montoison, 2025), and to any other solver in the JuliaSmoothOptimizers (JSO) ecosystem. The user side is what distinguishes ExaModels.jl. Each objective term and constraint is declared as an algebraic pattern paired with a data iterator, so a whole model is stated in a few such declarations. The declarations also keep the pattern–data separation intact for the evaluation machinery. Section 2.1 presents the solver-facing interface: the canonical NLP form and the meta-information accessors and callback functions that ExaModels.jl must supply. Section 2.2 presents the user-facing side: the SIMD abstraction, which preserves the partially separable, repetitive structure of large-scale NLPs, and the modeling syntax that builds models in that form. 

## 2.1 Solver-Facing Interface 

2.1.1 NLP Formulation 

The solver-side mathematical abstraction of the NLP for ExaModels.jl is as follows: min  

> x∈Rn

f (x; θ) s.t. g♭ ≤ g(x; θ) ≤ g♯, x♭ ≤ x ≤ x♯, (2) where x ∈ Rn is the decision vector, f (·) : Rn → R is the objective function, g(·) : Rn → Rm is the constraint function, and x♭, x ♯ ∈ Rn and g♭, g ♯ ∈ Rm are the variable and constraint bounds. The vector θ ∈ Rp collects 

problem parameters : quantities that the objective and constraints depend on but that are held fixed during the solve rather than optimized over. ExaModels.jl treats every model as such a parametric NLP by default; a model with no parameters is simply the special case p = 0. Throughout the paper, we assume that f (·)and g(·) are twice differentiable and well defined over the entire set defined by the bounds. The form (2) is canonical: any NLP can be written this way, and it is the form in which solvers expect the problem. As discussed in Section 1, an NLP solver does not consume a fixed set of problem data. Instead, it queries the modeling layer for numerical evaluations (Nocedal and Wright, 2006) at every iterate x it visits during the solve. The implementational abstraction splits the information the solver needs from ExaModels.jl into two kinds: static meta-information about the problem, supplied once, and per-iteration NLP function callbacks ,evaluated on demand. 

2.1.2 Meta-Information 

Before the solve begins, the solver queries static information about the problem, that is, data that is fixed throughout the solve and therefore supplied only once. ExaModels.jl stores this data in an 

NLPModels.NLPModelMeta object and exposes it through the NLPModels.jl meta-information accessors, such as NLPModels.get nvar and NLPModels.get ncon . The main entries are: 

• nvar , ncon , nnzj , nnzh : integer values for the problem dimensions, namely the numbers of variables and constraints and the numbers of Jacobian and Lagrangian Hessian nonzeros; 

10 • lvar /uvar , lcon /ucon , x0 , y0 : vector values that pin down the instance and its starting point, namely the variable bounds x♭, x ♯, the constraint bounds g♭, g ♯, and the initial primal point and Lagrange multipliers, both zero by default; 

• ifix , ilow , iupp , irng , ifree and jfix , jlow , jupp , jrng , jfree : index vectors partitioning the variables and constraints by bound type (fixed, lower-bounded, upper-bounded, range-bounded, and free); and 

• minimize : the optimization sense, that is, whether the problem is a minimization or a maximization. 

2.1.3 Callback Functions 

At each iterate, the solver queries the numerical values of the NLP functions through five callbacks. It supplies the iterate x and, for the Lagrangian Hessian, an objective weight and the multipliers. These callbacks constitute the solver-facing interface of ExaModels.jl. Except for the objective value, which is returned as a scalar, the callbacks are in-place (hence the ! suffix) and write their results into preallocated output arrays: 

• NLPModels.obj(model, x) : the objective value f (x) ∈ R;

• NLPModels.grad!(model, x, g) : the objective gradient ∇f (x) ∈ Rn, written into g;

• NLPModels.cons!(model, x, c) : the constraint values g(x) ∈ Rm, written into c;

• NLPModels.jac coord!(model, x, v) : the sparse constraint Jacobian ∇g(x) ∈ Rm×n, with nonzero values written into v;

• NLPModels.hess coord!(model, x, y, v; obj weight) : the sparse n × n Lagrangian Hessian 

σ∇2f (x) + Pmj=1 λj ∇2gj (x), with nonzero values written into v,where λ ∈ Rm is the vector of Lagrange multipliers, supplied in y, and σ is the objective weight, supplied as the keyword argument obj weight . Here and throughout the paper, gradients are column vectors, and the Jacobian of a vector-valued function stacks the transposed gradients of its components as rows. These five are the most commonly used callbacks; the NLPModels.jl interface additionally specifies others, such as: 

• NLPModels.jac structure!(model, I, J) : the COO row and column indices of the nonzero Jacobian entries, written into I and J;

• NLPModels.hess structure!(model, I, J) : the COO row and column indices of the nonzero Hessian entries, written into I and J;

• NLPModels.jprod! : the Jacobian–vector product; 

• NLPModels.jtprod! : the vector–Jacobian product; 

• NLPModels.hprod! : the Hessian–vector product. The index vectors are queried once, at the start of the solve. Together with the value vector v, they form the COO representation of the sparse derivative matrices, and only v is recomputed at each iterate. For the solver, the objective and constraint values are used to monitor progress (e.g., in the line search), and the first- and second-order derivatives are used to compute the step direction (e.g., the Newton step). Because they are evaluated at every iterate, these callbacks are performance-critical; providing them efficiently is a central goal of ExaModels.jl. This callback API was developed in NLPModels.jl by the JSO organization around the abstract type 

NLPModels.AbstractNLPModel . It decouples the modeling layer from the solver layer: any model that implements the interface is accepted by any compatible solver, including those listed at the opening of this section. ExaModel is a subtype of NLPModels.AbstractNLPModel and implements this callback interface. 

11 2.2 User-Facing Abstraction and Interface 

To provide the callbacks of Section 2.1, ExaModels.jl must store the information needed to evaluate the NLP functions. It stores that information in the form of the SIMD NLP formulation, keeping each repetitive pattern together with its data. 

2.2.1 SIMD NLP Formulation 

The SIMD NLP formulation captures the partially separable, repetitive structure of large-scale NLPs (Section 1) at the modeling level. Problems like the Goddard rocket (1) belong to the following class of NLPs, in which the repetitive patterns are kept explicit: min 

> x♭≤x≤x♯
> L

X

> ℓ=1
> Iℓ

X

> i=1

f (ℓ)(x; θ, p (ℓ) 

> i

) (3a) s.t. g♭ ≤

h

g(m)(x; θ, q (m) 

> j

)

iJm

> j=1

+

> Nm

X

> n=1
> Kn

X

> k=1

h(n)(x; θ, s (n) 

> k

) ≤ g♯, ∀ m ∈ { 1, . . . , M }, (3b) where x ∈ Rn is the vector of decision variables with bounds x♭, x ♯, and θ ∈ Rp is the problem-parameter vector of (2). The algebraic patterns f (ℓ) : Rn → R and g(m) : Rn → R are twice differentiable scalar functions of x, where these signatures suppress the dependence on θ and on the per-instance data. Each augmentation pattern h(n) : Rn → RJm is a vector-valued function whose output has a single nonzero entry. The position of that entry within constraint block m is fixed by the data s(n) 

> k

. The quantities p(ℓ) 

> i

, q(m) 

> j

, and s(n) 

> k

are problem data that parameterize each pattern instance. The vector θ, in contrast, is shared: the same parameter vector enters every pattern and every data point. The objective (3a) is split into L groups of objective patterns, where pattern f (ℓ) is evaluated over Iℓ data points. The constraints (3b) consist of M blocks: within block 

m, the “base” pattern g(m) defines Jm constraints, and the “augmentation” patterns h(n) contribute additive terms to these constraints. The augmentation structure arises naturally in network models, for example when arc flows are summed into nodal balance equations, and is discussed further in Section 3.1. Unlike (2), (3) is not a canonical form, since not every NLP is naturally written this way. It is, however, a form that appears throughout practice, as the examples below illustrate. Problems with partially separable, repetitive algebraic structure fit the form (3) with only a few distinct patterns; the Goddard rocket (1) is a direct instance, with the five patterns identified in Section 1, and the same holds across the problem categories surveyed there. We call the comprehensive interface built on this formulation the SIMD abstraction . It comprises the user-facing modeling syntax and the solver-facing evaluation machinery: the user writes the model in the form of (3), and the model data is stored in a structured form that exposes the repeated structure. This 

pattern–data separation is the foundation of the abstraction. Because the structure is partially separable, each pattern evaluation is independent across data indices, and the derivative computations inherit the same embarrassingly parallel structure. For a given pattern f (ℓ),the gradient contributions ∇xf (ℓ)(x; θ, p (ℓ) 

> i

) for i = 1 , . . . , I ℓ can be evaluated in parallel, and so can the Jacobian and Hessian contributions. By preserving the pattern structure at the modeling level, ExaModels.jl makes this parallelism directly available to the AD implementation. When the number of patterns is fixed and the problem size grows only through the number of data points, the evaluation of the NLP functions in principle attains O(1) parallel time complexity, given sufficiently many threads. The total work still grows with the number of data points; it is the parallel evaluation depth that stays constant. How the derivative computations are carried out in this parallel form, including the accumulation of overlapping contributions without race conditions, is presented in Section 5. The structure of (3) suggests how to store a model without losing its parameterized form. Rather than recording every scalar term one by one, each pattern is kept as a bundle of an instruction , the algebraic expression to evaluate, and the data over which that instruction is repeated. Because a large-scale NLP typically comprises only a few distinct patterns, the entire model reduces to a small number of such bundles. 

12 ExaModels.jl realizes the instruction as a SIMDFunction , which an Objective or a Constraint pairs with the matching data iterator. The model is therefore stored as a collection of SIMDFunction s, one per pattern, together with their data. Each callback then reduces to a loop over patterns that evaluates each pattern’s 

SIMDFunction at all of its data points. For the objective (3a), the value f (x) is the sum of the per-data-point evaluations, and the constraint and derivative callbacks follow the same structure. The internal construction of a SIMDFunction is presented in Sections 4 to 6. 

2.2.2 Modeling Syntax 

The syntax with which the user builds a model is shown on the Goddard rocket (1) of Section 1, in four stages: creating a model, solving it, accessing the solution, and updating the data and resolving. 

Creating a model. The variables, objective, and constraints are declared on an ExaCore : 

> 1

using ExaModels, NLPModelsIpopt  

> 23

# problem data  

> 4

nh = 200 # number of time steps  

> 5

h_0, v_0, m_0, g_0 = 1.0, 0.0, 1.0, 1.0  

> 6

T_c, h_c, v_c, m_c = 3.5, 500.0, 620.0, 0.6  

> 7

c_e = 0.5 * sqrt(g_0 * h_0)  

> 8

m_f = m_c * m_0  

> 9

D_c = 0.5 * v_c * (m_0 / g_0)  

> 10

T_max = T_c * m_0 * g_0  

> 11 12

# maximize final altitude  

> 13

core = ExaCore(minimize = false)  

> 14 15

# decision variables  

> 16

@add_var(core, h, 0:nh; start = 1.0, lvar = 1.0)  

> 17

@add_var(core, v, 0:nh; start = (i/nh * (1 - i/nh) for i = 0:nh))  

> 18

@add_var(core, m, 0:nh;  

> 19

start = ((m_f - m_0) * i/nh + m_0 for i = 0:nh),  

> 20

lvar = m_f, uvar = m_0)  

> 21

@add_var(core, tau, 0:nh; start = T_max/2, lvar = 0.0, uvar = T_max)  

> 22

@add_var(core, dt, 1; start = 1/nh, lvar = 0.0)  

> 23 24

# objective: final altitude  

> 25

@add_obj(core, h[nh])  

> 26 27

# altitude dynamics  

> 28

@add_con(core, alt,  

> 29

-h[i] + h[i-1] + 0.5 * dt[1] * (v[i] + v[i-1]) for i = 1:nh)  

> 30 31

# velocity dynamics  

> 32

@add_con(core, vel,  

> 33

-v[i] + v[i-1] + 0.5 * dt[1] * (  

> 34

(tau[i] - D_c * v[i]^2 * exp(-h_c * (h[i] - h_0) / h_0)  

> 35

- m[i] * g_0 * (h_0 / h[i])^2) / m[i]  

> 36

+ (tau[i-1] - D_c * v[i-1]^2 * exp(-h_c * (h[i-1] - h_0) / h_0)  

> 37

- m[i-1] * g_0 * (h_0 / h[i-1])^2) / m[i-1])  

> 38

for i = 1:nh)  

> 39 40

# mass dynamics 

13 41 @add_con(core, mass,  

> 42

-m[i] + m[i-1] + 0.5 * dt[1] * (-tau[i]/c_e - tau[i-1]/c_e) for i = 1:nh)  

> 43 44

# parameter: required final mass  

> 45

@add_par(core, mf, [m_f])  

> 46 47

# boundary conditions  

> 48

@add_con(core, h[0] - h_0) # initial altitude  

> 49

@add_con(core, v[0] - v_0) # initial velocity  

> 50

@add_con(core, m[0] - m_0) # initial mass  

> 51

@add_con(core, m[nh] - mf[1]) # final mass  

> 52 53

# instantiate: ExaCore -> ExaModel  

> 54

model = ExaModel(core) 

The patterns, namely the dynamics alt , vel , and mass , the boundary conditions, and the objective, correspond directly to (1). Each @add var call creates a block of variables rather than a single variable: h, for example, comprises one variable for each index in 0:nh . The same holds for the constraints: each @add con call creates a block of constraints, one per element of its data iterator (a singleton block when no iterator is given, as for the boundary conditions). By default, @add con declares an equality constraint, since lcon and ucon 

are both zero. An inequality constraint is declared by setting the keyword argument lcon or ucon . The final line instantiates the ExaCore as an ExaModel , the form in which the model is passed to the solver. The 

SIMDFunction s and their sparsity structure were already built as the patterns were added. The example contains no for loop outside the ExaModels.jl syntax. The objective and constraints are always declared as Julia generator expressions, body for i = iterator , with the loop written inside the macro call. We follow the Julia convention and call this construct the generator ; its iterator component is the 

data iterator . Because the data iterator is passed to the modeling backend rather than unrolled by the user, each declaration maps directly onto one pattern of (3), and the model is stored in the form of the SIMD abstraction. 

Solving. The model is passed to any NLPModels.jl-compatible solver:  

> 1

result = ipopt(model) # solve via Ipopt (NLPModelsIpopt.jl)  

> 2

result = madnlp(model) # solve via MadNLP.jl  

> 3

result = uno(model) # solve via Uno (UnoSolver.jl) 

Solving the problem on a GPU requires a GPU-compatible model. Such a model is obtained by replacing the first line of the example with core = ExaCore(minimize = false, backend = CUDABackend()) , with no other changes (Section 6). Passing that model to a GPU-compatible solver such as MadNLP.jl then solves the problem entirely on the GPU. Solvers in the JSO ecosystem return their results in a common template defined by SolverCore.jl. Here result is an instance of a subtype of SolverCore.AbstractExecutionStats . Its fields carry the solver status and the run statistics, together with the full primal solution ( result.solution ), the constraint multipliers ( result.multipliers ), and the bound multipliers ( result.multipliers L and 

result.multipliers U ). Each of these is a flat vector over all variables or over all constraints. The accessors below read from this object in the same way regardless of which solver produced it. 

Accessing the solution. From these flat vectors, the ExaModels.jl accessors slice out the part that belongs to a given block, using the block’s offset, and return it in the block’s shape:  

> 1

# primal: altitude trajectory  

> 2

h_sol = solution(result, h)  

> 34

# dual: velocity-dynamics multipliers 

14 5 lam = multipliers(result, vel) 

The variable-bound multipliers are read back the same way, via multipliers L(result, h) and 

multipliers U(result, h) .

Updating and resolving. Suppose that, after the solve, we wish to re-solve the problem with an updated value of the required final mass, held in the parameter mf . Such a change leaves the problem structure intact, so the numerical data can be modified in place and the model re-solved without rebuilding it. Here we raise the required final mass, tighten the thrust upper bound, and warm-start from the previous solution. Warm starting initializes the solver at a previously computed solution, primal or dual or both, so that a re-solve after a small change in the data converges in fewer iterations. The compiled SIMDFunction sare reused unchanged, and only the parameter, bound, and initial-point arrays are overwritten:  

> 1

# tighten max thrust  

> 2

set_uvar!(model, tau, fill(0.8 * T_max, nh + 1))  

> 34

# raise the required final mass  

> 5

set_value!(model, mf, [1.05 * m_f])  

> 67

# warm-start from previous solve  

> 8

set_start!(model, tau, solution(result, tau))  

> 9

result = ipopt(model) # resolve, no rebuild 

Because an ExaModel is a standard AbstractNLPModel , such warm-started resolves also work with any repeated-solve interface the chosen solver exposes. Some solvers reuse their internals across calls through more specialized interfaces, such as a persistent solver object with pre-allocated workspaces; for optimizing re-solve performance, we refer the reader to the documentation of the chosen solver. The example here demonstrates that the model object itself can be reused, which is most effective when successive solves leave the problem dimensions and the sparsity pattern unchanged. 

2.2.3 Model-Building Macros 

An ExaCore is a caching object: it accumulates the variables, parameters, objective patterns, and constraint patterns as the model is built. For the full reference to the macros that build an ExaCore , including every signature, keyword argument, and usage example, we refer the reader to the online documentation (Shin and contributors, 2025). Each macro takes the core as its first argument and rebinds it. For example, 

@add var(core, x, n) creates a variable block, binds the handle x to it, and reassigns core to the updated core; the other macros follow the same convention. The rebinding is needed because an ExaCore is immutable: each call produces a new core (Section 3.1). The macros are: 

• @add var(core, x, dims; start, lvar, uvar) : adds a variable block x with the given dimensions and optional initial values and bounds. 

• @add par(core, p, data) : adds a parameter block p whose values are appended to the parameter vector θ.

• @add obj(core, expr for i in data) : adds an objective pattern. The generator body is the ex-pression tree, and the generator’s iterator supplies the data. 

• @add con(core, expr for i in data; lcon, ucon) : adds a constraint pattern that defines a new block with one constraint per element of the data iterator, with bounds lcon and ucon .

• @add con!(core, con[j] += expr for (j, ...) in data) : augments an existing constraint block 

con with additive terms. This corresponds to the h(n) terms in (3b) and keeps the number of expression trees small. In network models, for instance, each contribution type such as generator injection or line flow becomes its own pattern, rather than being inlined into one large expression per constraint. 

15 • @add expr(core, s, expr for i in data) : adds a named, reusable subexpression block s that can be referenced as s[i] in later objectives and constraints. This lets the user name an intermediate quantity that recurs across several objectives or constraints, such as a nodal power injection that feeds both an objective term and several balance constraints. A reference s[i] inlines the subexpression’s tree into the consuming expression and introduces no auxiliary variables or constraints. Because the subexpression is itself a parameterized expression tree (Section 4), this keeps the model description compact. The reuse is a modeling convenience rather than a shared runtime computation, since each consuming pattern inlines its own copy of the tree (Section 7.1.1). 

2.2.4 Model Data Accessors 

Once the model is built, its numerical data can be read and modified one block at a time. In the accessors below, x, con , and param are the handles bound by the user’s variable, constraint, and parameter declarations (Section 2.2.2): 

• set start!(model, x, values) and get start(model, x) : the initial primal values of variable block 

x.

• set lvar!(model, x, values) /set uvar!(model, x, values) and get lvar(model, x) /

get uvar(model, x) : the variable bounds. 

• set lcon!(model, con, values) /set ucon!(model, con, values) and get lcon(model, con) /

get ucon(model, con) : the constraint bounds. 

• set value!(model, param, values) and get value(model, param) : the parameter values. Each accessor acts on a single variable, constraint, or parameter block, using the offset and dimension recorded in the block’s handle to address the relevant slice without copying. 

2.2.5 Solution Accessors 

The solution reported by the solver is a flat bundle: one vector each for the primal solution x, the constraint multipliers λ, and the bound multipliers, together with the solution status, the objective and constraint values, and solver statistics. The user, however, usually wants the portion that belongs to one declared variable or constraint block. ExaModels.jl therefore provides accessors that slice the flat vectors and return the entries of a single block, addressed by the declaration handles x and con and read from the solver’s result object introduced in the example above: 

• solution(result, x) : the primal solution of variable block x;

• multipliers(result, con) : the multipliers of constraint block con ;

• multipliers L(result, x) /multipliers U(result, x) : the lower- and upper-bound multipliers of variable block x.The per-block structure of the SIMD abstraction thus carries all the way through to how the user supplies data and reads back the solution. 

# 3 Core Data Structures 

The two central data structures in ExaModels.jl both store a model expressed in the SIMD abstraction (3) of Section 2.2 in a standardized form. An ExaCore is the immutable structure that holds the model as it is being built. An ExaModel is the immutable completed model, instantiated from a finished ExaCore , and it implements the NLPModels.jl callback interface of Section 2.1 (Figure 2). Inside these two containers sit the data types that represent the individual patterns of (3). Each objective pattern is stored as an Objective , each constraint block as a Constraint , and each additive contribution to an 

16 var , par : variable/parameter handles obj , cons : pattern tuples     

> x0 ,theta , bounds, and running structural counts backend ,tag ,refs

ExaCore       

> vars ,pars ,objs ,cons : tuples carried from the core
> theta : parameter values meta :NLPModelMeta counters : callback tallies ext : backend workspace

ExaModel ExaModel(core)          

> Solver
> obj ,grad! ,
> cons! ,
> jac coord! ,
> hess coord!
> callbacks
> Objective /Constraint /
> ConstraintAugmentation SIMDFunction : offsets
> o0,o1,o2,o1step,o2step ;
> comp1,comp2 AbstractNode ex-pression tree (see Figure 3) wraps holds tuple entries Each tuple entry realizes one algebraic pattern of the SIMD form (3): Objective ,
> Constraint , and ConstraintAugmentation for the objective, constraint, and augmen-tation patterns.

Figure 2: The construction-time and solver-facing containers. Arrows show what ExaModel(core) carries from one to the other, and which wrapper types sit inside both. existing constraint block as a ConstraintAugmentation . Each of the three wrappers holds a SIMDFunction 

and the data iterator over which the pattern is evaluated. The SIMDFunction in turn holds the parameterized expression tree for one algebraic pattern, together with the offsets and compressor maps that place its output. The expression tree itself, and how it is built, is the subject of Section 4. Both ExaCore and ExaModel are parametric structs . Their type parameters encode the floating-point type T,the array type VT , the backend B, and the concrete types of all stored variables, parameters, objectives, and constraints. For example, ExaCore {Float64, Vector {Float64 }, Nothing, ... } is a CPU model in double precision, and ExaCore {Float32, CuArray {Float32 }, CUDABackend, ... } is a single-precision model on an NVIDIA GPU. Because these choices are captured as type parameters, Julia’s compiler specializes the AD passes and solver callbacks for each concrete combination. A single codebase therefore handles CPU, CUDA, ROCm, oneAPI, and Metal backends with no runtime dispatch and no conditional branching in the evaluation path. The two structs follow a general principle: ExaModels.jl uses explicit, concrete typing throughout its performance-critical evaluation path. Every data structure on this path, from the individual expression-tree nodes up to the ExaCore and ExaModel that contain them, has a fully concrete Julia type. There are no abstractly typed fields, no type-unstable containers, and no runtime polymorphism during callback evaluation. Concrete types let the compiler specialize and inline the evaluation code completely, and they are also what make AOT compilation and GPU kernel generation possible (Section 7.3.1). The price is that even models of moderate complexity carry deeply nested type signatures. The typing of the expression trees is treated in Section 4.2.3. 

## 3.1 ExaCore: Model-Construction Cache 

An ExaCore is the construction-time cache of an optimization model. It accumulates the variables, parameters, algebraic patterns, numerical arrays, and structural counts needed to instantiate the eventual ExaModel . It is created by a call to ExaCore() , which optionally takes a GPU backend. The user then adds variables, parameters, objectives, and constraints with the macros @add var , @add par , @add obj , and @add con . The cache is immutable, so each addition returns an updated ExaCore . The reconstruction mechanics, and their role in preserving concrete types, are detailed in Section 7.1.1. Reflecting the explicit-typing principle, the struct is ExaCore {T, VT, B, S, V, P, O, C, ... }. Its key 

17 type parameters and fields are: 

• T and VT : the floating-point type and the array type, respectively, with VT constrained to 

AbstractVector {T}.

• backend::B : the KernelAbstractions.jl backend (e.g., CUDABackend() ) or nothing for CPU execution. 

• S: the model tag type, a typed extension point for carrying structural metadata without adding fields to the base core. For example, a two-stage core uses it to record scenario information and variable/constraint scenario assignments. 

• var::V : a tuple of Variable handles, each recording the block’s dimensions, its offset into the global variable vector x, its name, and a tag. 

• par::P : a tuple of Parameter handles, whose values are concatenated into the global parameter vector 

θ.

• obj::O : a tuple of Objective structs, each bundling a SIMDFunction and the collected data iterator. 

• cons::C : a tuple of Constraint and ConstraintAugmentation structs. A Constraint defines a new block of constraints with bounds. A ConstraintAugmentation , created via @add con! , adds terms to an existing constraint block without introducing new rows. 

• Primal, dual, parameter, and bound arrays, all of type VT : x0 , θ, lvar , uvar , y0 , lcon , and ucon .

• Running dimension counters (all Int ): nvar and npar count scalar variables and parameters; ncon 

counts constraint rows; nobj counts objective-term instances; and nconaug counts augmentation-term instances. 

• Running sparse-output counts (all Int ): nnzg counts objective-gradient contributions, nnzj counts constraint-Jacobian contributions, and nnzh counts Lagrangian-Hessian contributions. These counts determine output and workspace sizes and are updated as patterns are added. Each @add obj or @add con call builds its SIMDFunction at once, probing sparsity and building the compressor maps at the moment the pattern is registered. The updated ExaCore therefore already carries all the information needed for the final model. 

## 3.2 ExaModel: The Solver-Facing Model 

Once all variables, objectives, and constraints have been added, the user calls ExaModel(core) to instantiate the ExaCore as an immutable ExaModel . The ExaModel carries forward the core’s var , par , obj , and cons 

tuples as the fields vars , pars , objs , and cons , preserving their concrete types while placing them in a form ready for a solver. The instantiation performs four steps. 1. Constructs the NLPModelMeta object. It stores the problem dimensions n and m, the initial primal and dual iterates x0 and y0, the variable and constraint bounds x♭, x♯, g♭, g♯, the bound classification indices (free, lower-bounded, upper-bounded, fixed), and the nonzero counts nnzj and nnzh . This metadata follows the NLPModels.jl specification, and solvers query it to allocate sparse structures and to select algorithmic options. 2. Inherits the four tuples and the parameter values θ from the ExaCore .3. Builds the backend-specific extension ext when a GPU backend is active. This is the only step that analyzes the model. The gradient contributions of all patterns are collected, sorted by target variable, and partitioned by pointer arrays, which yields the sparse-to-dense compression map used by the GPU gradient path (Section 7.2.3). The same structure is built for constraint augmentations, and the device-side work buffers are allocated. The per-iteration callbacks can then launch with no further host-side preparation. On a CPU, no extension is built, and the instantiation reduces to reorganizing the data above. 

18 4. Initializes an NLPModels.Counters object to track callback invocations during the solve. The resulting ExaModel is a subtype of NLPModels.AbstractNLPModel {T,VT }, where T is the floating-point type and VT is the array type ( Vector {T} for CPU, CuArray {T} for CUDA, etc.). Its type has the form 

ExaModel {T, VT, E, V, P, O, C, ... }, where E is the type of the backend extension ext (Nothing on CPU) and V, P, O, C are the tuple types carried unchanged from the ExaCore . As with the core, a few further parameters for tags and references are not shown. An ExaModel can therefore be passed directly to any solver in the JSO ecosystem, including Ipopt (via NLPModelsIpopt.jl) and MadNLP.jl (Shin, Pacaud, and Montoison, 2025), without any adapter code. For each solver callback introduced in Section 2.1, ExaModels.jl dispatches to loops over the stored SIMDFunction s. 

## 3.3 Handles and Pattern Wrappers 

The internal types described below connect the two containers above to the expression trees of Section 4 and the AD machinery of Section 5. Most of them appear inside both the ExaCore and the ExaModel . They are the variable and parameter handles, the wrapper types stored in the obj and cons tuples, and the 

SIMDFunction that each wrapper holds. The node types underneath the SIMDFunction are the subject of Section 4. 

3.3.1 Variable and Parameter 

Each call to @add var or @add par declares a block of variables or parameters, possibly with multiple dimensions. Indices such as x[i] are local to the declared block, whereas solver callbacks operate on the global vectors x and θ formed by concatenating all variable and parameter blocks. Translating a block-local index into a global index therefore requires the block’s offset: if a block begins after offset scalar entries, its local entry i is stored at global index offset + i . The var and par tuples store lightweight handle types that record this mapping, rather than the numerical data itself. A Variable {S,O,T }, the handle returned by @add var , contains: 

• size::S : the block’s shape (a tuple of dimensions); general tensor blocks of arbitrary dimension are supported, with vectors and matrices as special cases. 

• length::O : the total number of scalar entries in the block. 

• offset::O : the offset that translates a block-local variable index into its index in the global primal vector x.

• name::Symbol : the variable’s name, captured automatically from the identifier in @add var(core, x, ...) and making the handle accessible as core.x .

• tag::T : optional user metadata, e.g. a scenario or batch-instance identifier for the two-stage and batch models of Sections 7.4.2 and 7.4.3. Indexing the handle as x[i] inside a later expression produces the symbolic variable reference of Section 4, and solution(result, x) uses the offset and size to slice the block’s values out of the solution vector. A Parameter {S,O,T }, the handle returned by @add par , carries the same bookkeeping except for the name: 

• size::S , length::O : the block’s shape and number of entries; as with variables, general tensor blocks of arbitrary dimension are supported. 

• offset::O : the offset that translates a block-local parameter index into its index in the global parameter vector θ.

• tag::T : optional user metadata, as for Variable .

19 Parameter values can be updated with set value! without rebuilding the model. Both handles are pure bookkeeping: they carry offsets and sizes, not arrays, which is consistent with the array-free design of Section 4. The numerical data, such as x0 , lvar , uvar , and θ, are stored separately in the flat arrays of the ExaCore .Declaring a block also extends the corresponding numerical arrays by one entry per scalar in the block. A call to @add var appends the block’s initial values to x0 and its lower and upper bounds to lvar and uvar . Their defaults are 0, −∞ , and + ∞, respectively, and the user may replace each default with a scalar, array, or generator. Similarly, @add par appends the block’s values to the global parameter array θ, using 0 by default. 

3.3.2 Objective, Constraint, and ConstraintAugmentation 

Each entry in the objective and constraint tuples of an ExaCore or ExaModel is one of three wrapper types, each pairing a SIMDFunction with the data over which it is evaluated. The contents and role of the 

SIMDFunction are described in Section 3.4. These collections are stored as Julia tuples rather than arrays, so that the concrete type of every wrapper appears explicitly in the top-level ExaCore or ExaModel type, down to the type of its SIMDFunction and expression tree. For example, two objective patterns are represented by a heterogeneous tuple such as Tuple {Objective {F1,I1 }, Objective {F2,I2 }} , which preserves the distinct types F1 and F2 . A Vector {Objective }, by contrast, would erase those distinctions behind the abstract 

Objective type and require dynamic dispatch during evaluation. The var tuple follows the same principle. Because extending any of these tuples changes its type, each addition reconstructs the core with a new concrete top-level type, as detailed in Section 7.1.1. An Objective {F,I } corresponds to one objective pattern ˜f (ℓ) in (3a) and contains: 

• f::F : the SIMDFunction constructed from the pattern. 

• itr::I : the collected data iterator over which the pattern is evaluated. A Constraint {F,I,O,S,T } corresponds to one base constraint pattern ˜ g(m) in (3b) and adds bookkeeping for the block’s location: 

• f::F , itr::I : the SIMDFunction and the data iterator, as above. 

• offset::O : the block’s offset into the global constraint vector. 

• size::S : the block’s dimensions. 

• tag::T : optional user metadata, as for Variable .A ConstraintAugmentation {F,I,D,T } corresponds to an augmentation pattern ˜h(n) in (3b). Its 

SIMDFunction wraps an expression tree of the form idx => value , where the index expression selects which 

constraint row to augment and the value expression supplies the additive term: 

• f::F , itr::I : the SIMDFunction and the data iterator. 

• oa::Int : the base offset of this augmentation’s terms in the GPU augmentation buffer. Each term is first written to its own position in this buffer and is subsequently accumulated into its target constraint row. The target constraint block’s base row is instead encoded by f.o0 .

• dims::D : the dimensions of that original constraint block, used to map index expressions to rows. 

• tag::T : optional user metadata, as for Variable .To see why ConstraintAugmentation is needed, consider the active power balance constraint in ACOPF: 

X

> g∈G b

pg − pdb = X

> (b,j )∈E

pf 

> bj

+ X

> (j,b )∈E

pt

> jb

, ∀b ∈ B ,

20 where Gb is the set of generators at bus b, and pf 

> bj

and pt 

> jb

are the flows on the lines incident to b, each measured at the b end of its line. With line losses, the power entering a line at one end differs from the power leaving it at the other, so each end carries its own flow variable. Each flow variable is the power the bus sends into the line at its end, so every incident line contributes to the balance with the same sign, whether the bus is the from end or the to end of the line. Each bus has a different number of generators and incident lines, so the full expression cannot be written as a single parameterized tree evaluated over buses. The tree structure 

varies from bus to bus, which violates the SIMD requirement that all data points share the same tree. With @add con! , the constraint is instead built in layers:  

> 1

# base pattern: one row per bus  

> 2

@add_con(core, c_p, b.pd for b in bus_data)  

> 34

# generator injection  

> 5

@add_con!(core, c_p, g.bus =>-pg[g.i] for g in gen_data)  

> 67

# line flow (from and to ends)  

> 8

@add_con!(core, c_p, l.from =>pf[l.i] for l in line_data)  

> 9

@add_con!(core, c_p, l.to =>pt[l.i] for l in line_data) 

The accumulated residual is demand plus line flows minus generation, which is the zero-valued form of the balance equation above. Each @add con! call creates a separate ConstraintAugmentation with its own fixed-structure expression tree and its own data iterator. The augmentation mechanism accumulates all contributions into the correct constraint rows at evaluation time. Without it, the user would have to write one large expression per bus that inlines every generator and line contribution, which destroys the pattern–data separation and blocks SIMD and GPU parallelism. More broadly, @add con! applies whenever a constraint sums over heterogeneous sets. Reactive power balance in ACOPF has the same structure, as do nodal flow balance constraints in optimal control on networks. The augmentation mechanism makes the number of distinct patterns scale with the number of types of contributions (generator, line-from, line-to), not with the number of constraints or the network topology. 

## 3.4 SIMDFunction: The Unit of Callback Work 

The parameterized expression tree, described in Section 4, captures only the algebraic structure of a pattern. Its Var leaves record which variables the pattern reads (Section 4.2.2), but the tree knows nothing else about the surrounding NLP model. It does not know whether it contributes to the objective or to the constraints. It does not know which data iterator it will be evaluated over, where its values and derivative nonzeros belong in the global output arrays, or how repeated derivative contributions should be merged. Turning the tree into a unit of callback work therefore takes additional bookkeeping. A SIMDFunction {F,C1,C2 } attaches the output offsets, the per-data-point strides, and the sparse compressor mappings to the expression tree. The data iterator is held by the enclosing Objective or Constraint , which pairs it with the SIMDFunction . Constraints also carry their row and shape metadata. The SIMDFunction is built at model-construction time by probing the expression tree for sparsity and building the compressor maps. It is the unit of work for both CPU loops and GPU kernels (Section 6), and it contains: 

• f::F : the parameterized expression tree representing the algebraic pattern. Its node structure and construction are discussed in Section 4. 

• comp1::C1 , comp2::C2 : Compressor mappings from reverse-pass visits to unique sparse output entries. The first-order mapping comp1 is used for gradient/Jacobian entries, whereas the second-order mapping 

comp2 is used for Lagrangian Hessian entries. Their construction and role in merging repeated contributions within a pattern are described in Section 5.2.2. 

21 • o0 , o1 , o2 , o1step , o2step : offset and stride integers, one offset per derivative order. The offsets locate this pattern’s entries in the shared output arrays, namely the function values, the gradient and Jacobian nonzeros, and the Lagrangian Hessian nonzeros. The strides record how far the write position advances from one data point to the next. Together they let each pattern write directly into its own slice without collisions; the formula for the write position is given in Section 5.3. 

# 4 Parameterized Expression Tree 

During model creation, ExaModels.jl captures each user-supplied generator body as a single parameterized expression tree : a Julia data structure that represents one algebraic pattern of (3) and is evaluated at every data point of that pattern. The word parameterized refers to the data-point index i, which is left as an undetermined parameter. Each leaf that depends on the data point records only how to fetch its value once i is known, such as which variable index or which data field, and not the value itself. Supplying a concrete i at evaluation time instantiates the tree for that data point by a forward pass, without copying it or allocating a new one. Keeping the decision variables symbolic is standard in symbolic expression-tree generation. Extending the same treatment to the data-point index is not standard, and it is what lets one tree serve an entire family of expressions rather than one tree per scalar expression. For a constraint pattern g(m) in (3b), the algebraic template becomes the expression tree stored by a 

SIMDFunction . The data points q(m) 

> j

become the data iterator itr stored by the enclosing Constraint , and objective patterns are represented in the same way by Objective . Evaluating a constraint row instantiates this shared tree at the corresponding element i of itr . The remaining ingredients of the SIMDFunction ,namely the compressor maps and the output offsets, are deferred to Section 5. The defining design decision is to keep the array data entirely separate from the tree. The tree refers to decision variables, parameters, and data-point fields only symbolically, by the index or access path needed to fetch each value. It therefore carries no array data of its own and is fully immutable. This is what lets the explicit typing of Section 3 reach every node. Each node type is parameterized by the types of its children, so nesting produces a tree whose type encodes the node itself and its entire subtree, and the type of the root alone determines the algebraic structure of the pattern. The compiler can then specialize a dispatch-free evaluator for each pattern. The tree is also a bits type, because it is array-free and concretely typed, so it can be passed directly as a GPU kernel argument, with the numerical data supplied alongside as device arrays (Section 6). 

## 4.1 Tree Construction from the Modeling Syntax 

A parameterized expression tree is not a distinct data structure but a composition of nested nodes, each holding its children as fields. The user never writes those nodes. They are built incrementally by Julia’s multiple dispatch as the ordinary expression in an @add obj or @add con declaration (Section 2.2.2) is evaluated. Each operation in the expression wraps the nodes its operands have already become, so the tree grows outward from the leaves as evaluation proceeds. Section 4.2 catalogues the node types in full. 

4.1.1 Building Nodes from the Bottom Up 

Construction starts from the source nodes , which stand in for the vectors and the data point that a pattern is written against. They are VarSource for the decision variables x, ParameterSource for the parameters θ,and DataSource for the current data point. Indexing or field access on a source node does not read a value. It returns an indexed node that records how the value will be fetched once a data point is supplied:  

> 1

julia> x = VarSource(); θ= ParameterSource(); i = DataSource();  

> 23

julia> x[2]

x[2]

> 12

> 13

> 14 15

> 16

> 17

i.a 

A node prints as the mathematical expression it stands for, with the decision-variable vector, the parameter vector, and the data point shown as x, θ, and i. Those names are fixed by the printer rather than taken from the variable a source is bound to, so we bind the sources to the same names here. The expression 

x[2] invokes getindex(x, 2) . Because x is a VarSource , multiple dispatch selects getindex(::VarSource, ...) and returns a Var node instead of reading an array element. The indices used so far have been literals. The parameterization enters when an indexed node itself serves as the index of a variable or a parameter.  

> 1

julia> x[i[1]]  

> 2

> 45

> 6

> 7

θ[i.a] 

Neither expression names a particular entry of x or θ. Each records the access path by which the entry will be determined once a data point is supplied. A single node therefore stands for a different variable at every data point, and one tree serves an entire pattern. This is what the word parameterized refers to, and it is the property that the rest of the construction preserves. These indexed nodes are the ingredients of larger expressions. Applying an ordinary Julia function or operator to one builds a further node rather than computing a number, because each registered function (Section 7.1.4) carries an overload for AbstractNode operands. The results are the general nodes Node1 and Node2 , for unary and binary operations respectively:  

> 1

julia> x[2]^2  

> 2

Node1{abs2}  

> 3

x[2]^2  

> 45

julia> sin(x[2])  

> 6

> 89

> 10

> 11

julia> x[i[1]] * θ[i[2]]  

> 14

Node2{*}  

> 15

x[i[1]] * θ[i[2]] 

23 In each case the function or operator itself is carried in the node’s first type parameter, so the algebraic form of the pattern is fixed at compile time. Applied repeatedly, this yields the complete parameterized expression tree. 

4.1.2 From a Model Declaration to a Tree 

The construction above proceeded upward, one expression at a time, with the nodes written out by hand. From a single @add obj or @add con statement in the modeling syntax of Section 2.2.2, the same machinery produces the tree for a whole pattern without the user naming a single node. Consider the extended Rosenbrock function: min 

> x∈RN
> N

X

> i=2

100( x2 

> i−1

− xi)2 + ( xi−1 − 1) 2. (4) In ExaModels.jl, this is specified as a single objective pattern:  

> 1

@add_obj(core, 100(x[i-1]^2 - x[i])^2 + (x[i-1] - 1)^2 for i in 2:N) 

Julia lowers the generator into a one-argument function f = i -> 100*(x[i-1]^2 - x[i])^2 + (x[i-1] -1)^2 , paired with the range 2:N that supplies its data points. The name x inside the body is the variable handle declared by @add var , which the function captures from the enclosing scope. To construct a single expression tree for this pattern, ExaModels.jl calls f once, with i = DataSource() . This one source-node argument turns f into a probe of its own algebraic structure: each operation builds a symbolic node. The tree is built step by step through multiple dispatch. When the generator body is evaluated with this source node: 1. i - 1 dispatches to -(::AbstractNode, ::Real) , returning a Node2 with the DataSource and the constant as children; 2. x[i-1] dispatches to getindex on the variable handle x. This appends the offset of the variable block within the global decision vector, which is zero in this example, and returns a Var node owning the resulting index subtree; 3. x[i-1]^2 lowers to Base.literal pow with the exponent carried as Val {2}, applying the simplification rule ( ·)2 → abs2( ·) and returning a Node1 {abs2,... };4. the subtraction, outer square, multiplication by 100, and addition each dispatch similarly, constructing 

Node2 nodes. No loop is unrolled during model construction: the final result is a single expression tree whose Var leaves resolve variable indices from the data point at evaluation time. The same tree is evaluated for each 

i ∈ { 2, . . . , N }, with different data producing different variable indices and hence different function values and derivatives. This is the parameterized aspect: one tree serves all N − 1 data points, and its evaluation functions are compiled only once. It is the key to SIMD evaluation and GPU acceleration. The result is a single value whose type spells out the entire algebraic structure of the pattern. That type is long, so ExaModels.jl abbreviates it when a node is displayed. The fulltype function prints it in full:
