Every operation, every operand, and the index arithmetic inside each Var appear in the type, which is what allows the compiler to specialize the evaluation of this pattern (Section 4.2.3). Figure 3 shows the expression tree for this pattern. General nodes carry their operation as a type parameter, and source and constant nodes supply the inputs. Each Var node contains a subtree that computes the variable index from the data point i. The reference x[i-1] becomes a Var node whose index subtree combines the DataSource leaf with two integer constants and evaluates ( i − 1) + 0. The trailing constant is the offset of the variable block x within the global decision vector, which is zero in this example. At evaluation time the subtree resolves to the index i − 1, so the same tree evaluates 100( x21 − x2)2 + ( x1 − 1) 2 when i = 2, 100( x22 − x3)2 + ( x2 − 1) 2 when i = 3, and so on for the remaining data points. 

4.1.3 Forms of the Data Iterator 

The model declarations so far iterate over 2:N , the obvious choice when a pattern is indexed by an integer. The data iterator is not restricted to a range. It may be any collection whose elements the pattern accesses, because those accesses are captured symbolically. The getindex , getproperty , and indexed iterate methods on 

DataSource all return DataIndexed nodes, so an element may be addressed by position or by field name. The elements must be concretely typed, so that the access paths resolve at compile time. For GPU execution they must additionally be bits types, since the data iterator is copied to the device and the kernel receives it 

25 as an argument. A tuple carries the data by position, and the element may be indexed or destructured:  

> 1

data = [(100.0, k-1, k) for k in 2:N]  

> 23

@add_obj(core, d[1]*(x[d[2]]^2 - x[d[3]])^2 + (x[d[2]] - 1)^2 for d in data)  

> 4

@add_obj(core, c*(x[i]^2 - x[j])^2 + (x[i] - 1)^2 for (c, i, j) in data) 

The two declarations produce the same tree: destructuring dispatches to indexed iterate on the DataSource ,which returns the same DataIndexed nodes that explicit indexing does. A named tuple carries the data by name instead, which is clearer when a pattern has several fields:  

> 1

data = [(coef = 100.0, i = k-1, j = k) for k in 2:N]  

> 23

@add_obj(core, d.coef*(x[d.i]^2 - x[d.j])^2 + (x[d.i] - 1)^2 for d in data) 

A user-defined struct behaves exactly as the named tuple does, since the field accesses resolve through the same getproperty overload:  

> 1

struct Arc  

> 2

coef::Float64  

> 3

i::Int  

> 4

j::Int  

> 5

end  

> 67

data = [Arc(100.0, k-1, k) for k in 2:N]  

> 89

@add_obj(core, a.coef*(x[a.i]^2 - x[a.j])^2 + (x[a.i] - 1)^2 for a in data) 

Nested combinations of these forms work in the same way. In each case the field name or position becomes part of the DataIndexed type parameter, so the access path is fixed at compile time and involves no runtime lookup. 

## 4.2 Node Types 

The node types fall into three families: the leaves, which either stand for the vectors and the data point or carry a constant value; the indexed nodes obtained from the source nodes; and the general nodes that apply registered functions and operators. 

4.2.1 Leaf Nodes 

The leaves are of two kinds: the source nodes , which record where a value will be fetched once a data point is supplied, and constants, which carry a value directly. 

• VarSource : the decision-variable vector x. Indexing it produces a Var node. 

• ParameterSource : the parameter vector θ. Indexing it produces a ParameterNode , and it appears only in models that carry parameters (Section 2.1). 

• DataSource : the current data point. It admits field access as well as indexing, so a structured data point can be addressed by name, and either form produces a DataIndexed node. 

• Real : a literal coefficient is not wrapped at all. It is carried as a plain Julia number in the child field of the general node above it, which is why a general node’s children are typed as AbstractNode or Real .

• Constant {V}: a constant that carries its value V in its type rather than in a field, so the value is available at compile time (Section 7.1.3). 

26 VarSource and ParameterSource exist only to be indexed; a pattern never refers to the whole vector x

or θ at once. A DataSource is different: when the data point is itself the quantity a pattern needs, it is used directly rather than indexed. The Rosenbrock declaration of Section 4.1.2 is such a case, since its data iterator 2:N yields plain integers and the pattern uses i as an index into x rather than reading a field of it. The Var , ParameterNode , and DataIndexed nodes that indexing produces are the subject of Section 4.2.2. 

4.2.2 Indexed Nodes 

The indexed nodes produced from the source nodes are: 

• Var {I}: a decision-variable access x[i] , where the index type I may be a literal or a data-dependent subtree, so the index itself is computed from the data point. 

• DataIndexed {I,J }: the lookup inner.J or inner[J] , where I is the type of the inner node being indexed into and J is the field name or index itself. Indexing the data point i at position 1 gives 

DataIndexed {DataSource, 1 }, and accessing its field a gives DataIndexed {DataSource, :a }. The inner node may itself be a DataIndexed , so a nested access path such as i.a[2] is expressed by nesting the type, as DataIndexed {DataIndexed {DataSource, :a }, 2 }. Since J is carried in the type , the access compiles to a direct getfield or getindex with no runtime lookup. 

• ParameterNode {I}: a model-parameter access θ[i] , produced by indexing the ParameterSource leaf. As with Var {I}, the index type I may be a literal or a data-dependent subtree, so a parameter entry can also be selected by the data point. 

4.2.3 General Nodes 

The general nodes come in two forms, distinguished by the number of arguments: 

• Node1 {F,I }: a univariate operation f with F = typeof(f) and a single child subtree of type I,evaluating as f(inner(i, x, θ)) ;

• Node2 {F,I1,I2 }: a bivariate operation f with F = typeof(f) and two child subtrees of types I1 , I2 ,evaluating as f(inner1(i, x, θ), inner2(i, x, θ)) .ExaModels.jl supports only univariate and bivariate functions as building blocks, so an n-ary operation such as a sum of several terms is represented as a chain of nested nodes rather than a single wide node. The type parameters are what make this representation more than a bookkeeping device. The operation is carried in F, so two nodes with different operations are different concrete types, and the child types I (or I1 ,

I2 ) record the subtrees in full. Together with the parameters carried by Var {I}, DataIndexed {I,J }, and 

Constant {V}, the entire nesting structure of an expression is therefore part of its type. The expression x2

> i−1

,for instance, has a type of the form 

Node1 {typeof(abs2), Var {Node2 {typeof(+), ... }}} .This is the explicit-typing principle of Section 3 carried furthest: a complete pattern is one deeply nested concrete type, with no abstractly typed field anywhere on the evaluation path. The compiler can therefore inline and specialize the evaluation code for each pattern. Different patterns are different types, so it produces a separate specialized routine for each, which is what makes SIMD evaluation and GPU kernel compilation possible. 

4.2.4 The Root 

The outermost node of the tree is called the root , but it is not a distinct node type. It is simply the general, indexed, or constant node that represents the complete algebraic pattern and is stored in the f field of the 

SIMDFunction (Section 3.3). Its role is to be the entry point: evaluating the pattern at a data point means calling the root, which calls its children in turn. Section 4.3 describes that traversal. 

27 4.3 Node Evaluation 

Every node <: AbstractNode is callable with the standard evaluation arguments, node(i, x, θ), and the call returns a scalar. Here i is the data point, that is, the element of the data iterator, x is the variable vector, and θ is the parameter vector, which is unused when the model carries no parameters. Every node, and hence every expression tree, is evaluated through calls of this form. For example, a tree can be constructed and evaluated directly, and the same tree returns a different value at each data point:  

> 1

julia> x = VarSource(); i = DataSource();  

> 23

julia> node = 100 * (x[i-1]^2 - x[i])^2 + (x[i-1] - 1)^2;  

> 45

julia> node(2, [2.0, 3.0, 5.0], nothing)  

> 6

101.0  

> 78

julia> node(3, [2.0, 3.0, 5.0], nothing)  

> 9

1604.0 

Arithmetic on the source nodes assembles the tree by operator overloading, and each call evaluates that one tree at the given data point and variable values. The data point selects which entries of x the Var leaves read, which is why the two calls return different values. This tree reads no parameters, so the third argument is unused and may be passed as nothing . Evaluation descends from the root to the leaves: each general node evaluates its children and applies its operation, while the leaves read from the arguments. A Var node, for example, reads its entry of x, and a DataIndexed node extracts its field or entry from the data point i.At the individual node level, evaluation applies the function associated with the node type to the recursively evaluated children. For example, for every registered bivariate function f, the registration macro (Section 7.1.4) generates the Node2 evaluation method  

> 1

@inline (n::Node2{typeof(f),I1,I2})(i, x, θ) where {I1,I2} =  

> 2

f(n.inner1(i, x, θ), n.inner2(i, x, θ)) 

so calling a Node2 first evaluates its two child subtrees inner1 and inner2 through the same interface and then applies f to their values. A Node1 evaluates in the same way with a single child. When a child is a plain 

Real coefficient, a specialized method uses the stored value directly instead of recursing. Values therefore flow back up from the leaves to the root, and the call on the root returns the instantiation of the tree at the given data entry i, variables x, and parameters θ.Every use of the tree goes through this same call; only the type of the arguments varies. Called with numerical arguments, it computes the function value, which is the regular evaluation path used by the objective and constraint callbacks. Called with a derivative-carrying adjoint source in place of x, the identical traversal builds the computation graph from which the derivatives are evaluated, for both first- and second-order derivatives. This is the basis of the forward pass of the AD algorithm of Section 5. 

# 5 Sparse Automatic Differentiation 

The same evaluation call node(i, x, θ) that instantiates the tree also produces derivatives, once x is replaced by an input type that carries derivative information. The operator overloads then propagate derivatives through the same expression structure. The derivative evaluation itself (Section 5.1) is standard reverse-mode AD. The one difference is that it runs on the parameterized expression tree, so the same compiled code serves every data point of a pattern and the data points can be processed in parallel (Section 6). Second derivatives are obtained by reverse over reverse, rather than by the forward-over-reverse composition that machine learning frameworks generally use. 

28 The larger departure from common practice is in how the sparse Jacobian and Hessian are obtained. The usual route in a machine learning framework is to expose Jacobian–vector and Hessian–vector products. The sparse matrix is recovered from a sequence of such products, and the sequence is chosen by a graph coloring of the sparsity pattern. ExaModels.jl instead evaluates the nonzero entries directly, in a single pass per pattern. The sparsity pattern is determined a priori from the expression tree (Section 5.2), so no matrix–vector products are formed and no coloring is computed. 

## 5.1 Derivative Evaluation 

We focus on the first-order case, the gradient. The second-order case follows the same structure and is described at the end of this subsection. Consider one term of the extended Rosenbrock function (4), written in the two variables it involves, 

f (x1, x 2) = 100( x21 − x2)2 + ( x1 − 1) 2,

evaluated through the intermediates 

s = x21 − x2, t = x1 − 1, f = 100 s2 + t2.

Reverse-mode AD associates with every intermediate quantity v an adjoint ¯v = ∂f /∂v , the sensitivity of the output to that intermediate. The adjoint of the output itself is ¯f = 1. The chain rule then propagates adjoints backward through each elementary operation. If v = F (u1, u 2), each argument uj receives the contribution ¯v · ∂F/∂u j , the adjoint of the result times the local partial derivative of the operation. In the example, the backward traversal proceeds as ¯f = 1 , ¯s = 200 s, ¯t = 2 t, 

¯x1 = ¯ s · 2x1 + ¯ t · 1 = 400 x1(x21 − x2) + 2( x1 − 1) , ¯x2 = −¯s = −200( x21 − x2),

which are the entries of ∇f . A single backward traversal of these adjoints therefore yields the full gradient at a cost proportional to one function evaluation. ExaModels.jl follows this standard scheme, in the same two phases: a forward pass that evaluates the parameterized expression tree of Section 4 and records the local derivatives along the way, and a reverse pass 

that propagates adjoints from the root back to the leaves. 

5.1.1 Forward Pass 

The forward pass uses the node-evaluation machinery of Section 4.3. It is the ordinary evaluation call node(i, x, θ), with an AdjointNodeSource passed in place of x. No separate symbolic construction takes place. The evaluation produces the adjoint tree: a structure with the same shape as the original expression tree, in which each node carries its primal value and its local partial derivatives. The construction reuses the multiple dispatch mechanism of the primal tree, with the local derivative F ′ supplied by the function’s registered first derivative (Section 7.1.4). The first-order source AdjointNodeSource is a thin wrapper around the primal vector x. The pass evaluates the stored tree as node(i, AdjointNodeSource(x), θ). Indexing that source returns an AdjointNodeVar(i, x[i]) leaf, a variable node that also carries its primal value. As the operator overloads run on these leaves, each interior operation builds an AdjointNode1 or AdjointNode2 node (both <: AbstractAdjointNode )storing, alongside the primal value, the local partial derivatives of its operation: 

• AdjointNodeVar {I,T }: stores the variable index i and the primal value xi.

• AdjointNode1 {F,T,I }: for a unary F , stores v = F (u) and ∂F/∂u = F ′(u), where u is the child’s primal value. 

• AdjointNode2 {F,T,I1,I2 }: for a binary F , stores v = F (u1, u 2) and both partial derivatives ∂F/∂u 1,

∂F/∂u 2.

29 The second-order case is identical in structure: a SecondAdjointNodeSource seeds SecondAdjointNodeVar 

leaves, and the overloads build <: AbstractSecondAdjointNode nodes that also store second derivatives (Section 5.1.3). In both cases the adjoint tree is built during the forward pass and traversed back during the reverse pass. Figure 4 illustrates both passes on the Rosenbrock pattern. Evaluating x21x2 on an AdjointNodeSource returns the root of an adjoint tree rather than a number. Its fields hold the value of the subexpression and the local partial derivatives with respect to its children:  

> 1

julia> node = x[1]^2 * x[2];  

> 23

julia> g = node(nothing, AdjointNodeSource([3.0, 5.0]), nothing);  

> 45

julia> g.x, g.y1, g.y2 # value, and the two local partials  

> 6

(45.0, 5.0, 9.0)  

> 78

julia> g.inner1.x, g.inner1.y # the x[1]^2 child: value and d/dx[1]  

> 9

(9.0, 6.0) 

No global derivative has been computed at this point: each node knows only its own value and how it responds to its immediate children. Combining these local factors into the gradient is the work of the reverse pass. 

5.1.2 Reverse Pass 

The reverse pass traverses the adjoint tree from root to leaves, propagating the adjoint (sensitivity) ¯ v. The traversal is implemented by a family of reverse-pass functions that share this structure and differ only in where the results are written: drpass (dense gradient), grpass (sparse gradient), jrpass (sparse Jacobian), and the second-order hrpass and hdrpass (sparse Hessian). Each is discussed where it is used. Taking 

drpass as the representative: 

• At the root, ¯ v is set to the seed adjoint: 1 for the gradient and the Jacobian, and the multiplier λj of constraint j for the Hessian of the Lagrangian. 

• At AdjointNode1 {F} with stored derivative F ′: propagate ¯ v · F ′ to the child. 

• At AdjointNode2 {F} with stored partials ∂1F , ∂2F : propagate ¯ v · ∂1F to child 1 and ¯ v · ∂2F to child 2. 

• At AdjointNodeVar with index i: accumulate ¯ v into the gradient entry ( ∇f )i.Returning to the pattern above, the reverse pass multiplies these local factors along every path from the root and accumulates the products at the leaves:  

> 1

julia> y = zeros(2);  

> 23

julia> drpass(g, y, 1.0) # 1.0 is the seed adjoint at the root  

> 45

julia> y  

> 6

2-element Vector{Float64}:  

> 7

30.0  

> 8

9.0 

matching ∇(x21x2) = (2 x1x2, x 21) = (30 , 9) at x = (3 , 5). Here the gradient is dense and each leaf writes to its own variable index. For the sparse Jacobian and Hessian, the reverse pass writes instead into the nonzero positions of a coordinate (COO) array, which presupposes that those positions are already known. How they are determined, without graph coloring, is the subject of Section 5.2. 

30 5.1.3 Second-Order Derivatives 

The Hessian of the Lagrangian requires second-order derivatives, which are propagated in the same fashion as the first-order case. The expression tree is evaluated with SecondAdjointNodeSource(x) , producing a second-adjoint tree whose nodes also store the second derivatives of their operations ( h = F ′′ for unary nodes; 

h11 , h12 , h22 for binary nodes). The reverse pass then propagates the pair of first and second adjoints (¯ v, ¯v2)from root to leaves. At a unary node with y = F ′ and h = F ′′ , the child receives ¯ v · y and ¯ v2 · y2 + ¯ v · h,which is the work of hrpass . The companion hdrpass traverses pairs of subtrees and accumulates the mixed contributions ¯ v2 · y1y2 + ¯ v · h12 . At the leaves, hrpass accumulates ¯ v2 into the diagonal Hessian entry Hi,i .The function hdrpass accumulates into the entry Hi,j when its two subtrees reach variable leaves with indices 

i and j. The second-derivative work is skipped on affine nodes, whose h terms are zero, as described in Section 7.2.2. 

5.1.4 Worked Example: Gradient of the Rosenbrock Pattern 

Figure 4 recalls the parameterized tree of Figure 3 for the pattern of (4) and instantiates it at a concrete data point. For i = 2 and x = [2 , 3, . . . ] the variable references resolve to x1 and x2, and the tree evaluates to 

f2 = 101. We let s = x2

> i−1

and F ′ = 2 xi−1.The subtraction node s = x2

− xi stores ∂1 = 1 and ∂2 = −1. The outer abs2 stores v = s2 and F ′ = 2 s.The multiplication by 100 stores ∂1 = s2 and ∂2 = 100. The t = xi−1 − 1 and t2 nodes store their partials in the same way. The root + node holds the primal value v = 100 s2 + t2.The reverse pass then traverses the same tree from the root down (Figure 4), seeding the root adjoint ¯ v = 1 and multiplying by each node’s stored local derivative as it descends: 1. the root + sends ¯ v · 1 = 1 to each of its two children (the 100 s2 branch and the t2 branch); 2. at the multiplication node the 100 s2 branch receives 1 · 100 = 100, which the outer abs2 scales by

−200 s, which is the gradient of fi. At the data point of Figure 4, where s = t = 1 and x1 = 2, these are 

∂f 2/∂x 1 = 802 and ∂f 2/∂x 2 = −200. 

## 5.2 Sparsity Detection Without Coloring 

Sparse Jacobian and Hessian evaluation is commonly implemented with graph coloring (Gebremedhin, Manne, and Pothen, 2005). Structurally independent columns are grouped into colors, so that the whole derivative is recovered from one compressed evaluation per color. This is the mechanism behind the sparse AD of general modeling and machine learning frameworks, among them ADNLPModels.jl (Migot et al., 2025) in the JSO ecosystem and the coloring-based sparse Jacobian and Hessian routines available for JAX and PyTorch. All of them first detect a sparsity pattern, color it, and then evaluate one matrix–vector product per color. The number of evaluations is therefore the chromatic number of the pattern, which the framework must compute. The entries must afterwards be scattered from the compressed result back to their positions in the sparse matrix. 

31 Parameterized tree

Green marks the forward pass and its stored local derivatives; maroon dashed arrows mark the reverse pass.

32 ExaModels.jl takes a different route. The SIMD abstraction fixes the structure of each pattern a priori at model-construction time. The exact structural sparsity layout can therefore be determined without coloring, before any derivative is computed. The reverse pass then writes each nonzero straight to its position in the COO array. No coloring is computed, no compressed evaluation is performed, and no scatter is needed. The entries so produced are not fully deduplicated. A variable that appears at more than one data point, or in more than one pattern, contributes a separate entry for each, and the solver sums them when it assembles the matrix. This is not peculiar to our route, since a coloring-based evaluation likewise produces a compressed array from which the entries must afterwards be recovered. The bookkeeping for this layout takes place at the SIMDFunction level. The stored expression tree serves as the template of the pattern’s algebraic structure, the Compressor merges repeated visits to the same variable into unique nonzero entries, and the offsets place each pattern’s entries in the global arrays. 

5.2.1 Probing the Tree with Sentinel Inputs 

For each pattern, ExaModels.jl performs a single probe evaluation of the expression tree using sentinel 

arguments: placeholder sources that stand in for the ordinary inputs. For the Rosenbrock pattern of (4), with 

obj the created Objective and f = obj.f.f its lifted function, the first-order probe proceeds as follows in the Julia REPL:  

> 1

julia> d = f(Identity(),  

> 2

AdjointNodeSource(NaNSource{Float64}()),  

> 3

NaNSource{Float64}());  

> 45

julia> raw = Any[];  

> 67

julia> grpass(d, nothing, nothing, nothing, raw, NaN);  

> 89

julia> raw  

> 10

3-element Vector{Any}:  

> 11

x[i - 1]  

> 12

x[i]  

> 13

x[i - 1] 

The primal vector x and the parameter vector θ are replaced by NaNSource {T} sentinels, and the data point 

i by an Identity sentinel. The numerical values these return are immaterial, since the probe records only which entries are touched. NaN is used so that any result that did depend on one would be visibly poisoned rather than plausible. Under these sentinels, the data-access nodes ( DataSource , DataIndexed ) evaluate to 

NaN , since no real data is available, while the Var nodes record which symbolic variable references are visited. The probe runs the same reverse-pass infrastructure used for ordinary derivative evaluation, grpass for first order and hrpass0 for second order. The only difference is at the leaves. With the Compressor argument set to nothing , a separate method makes each variable leaf record the symbolic variable reference it resolved, instead of accumulating a numeric value. Because the probe reuses that same infrastructure, every general node is visited in the identical order. The recorded sequence of nonzero positions therefore matches, entry for entry, the order in which the numeric passes will later write them. The three entries displayed above are the symbolic variable references visited in order: the first two from the term 100( x2 

> i−1

− xi)2 and the third from (xi−1 − 1) 2.

5.2.2 Compressor: Merging Repeated Leaf Visits 

The sequence raw collected by the probe may contain duplicates, for instance when xi appears more than once in the expression. Without merging them, the number of stored nonzeros would grow with the number of leaf visits rather than with the number of unique entries. The Compressor is a mapping from the position in raw to the unique nonzero index: 

33 1. Compute the list of unique variable references (for the gradient and Jacobian) or unique ( i, j ) pairs (for the Hessian) from raw .2. Build a mapping tuple: for each entry of raw , record which unique index it corresponds to. 3. Store this mapping in a Compressor {I}, whose type parameter I is an NTuple {N,Int } and so fixes the length N at compile time. Because the length is fixed in the type, looking up comp(cnt) is an inlined, constant-time operation with no runtime allocation. Continuing the Rosenbrock example in the REPL, the recorded sequence contains two unique references, which determine o1step = 2:  

> 1

julia> unique(raw)  

> 2

2-element Vector{Any}:  

> 3

x[i - 1]  

> 4

x[i] 

and mapping each position of raw to the unique entry it corresponds to gives the stored compressor:  

> 1

julia> obj.f.comp1  

> 2

Compressor{Tuple{Int64, Int64, Int64}}((1, 2, 1)) 

The first and third leaf visits accumulate into the first unique nonzero, and the second visit into the second. The second-order probe records four entries over three unique nonzeros, the two diagonal entries and one off-diagonal, yielding Compressor((1, 2, 3, 1)) with o2step = 3. Throughout, only the lower triangle (i ≥ j) of the symmetric Hessian is stored, following the NLPModels.jl convention. The resulting Jacobian and Hessian are stored in a partially compressed coordinate format, and the qualifier 

partially reflects where uniqueness has been resolved. At the level of an individual expression tree, the 

Compressor has already resolved it: duplicate entries arising from the same tree, such as those from a variable that appears more than once in one expression, are merged. Duplicates can still occur across different expression trees, and we deliberately do not compress across different patterns or across different data points of the same pattern. If two patterns contribute to the same ( i, j ) entry of the Hessian, they write to separate positions in the COO array. The final matrix is therefore only partially compressed, and a solver that requires a fully compressed format, such as compressed sparse column (CSC), must perform the remaining compression during its sparse matrix assembly. This design choice has two benefits. First, different patterns, and different data points within a pattern, never write to the same memory location, which removes write conflicts and allows lock-free parallel evaluation. Second, the same compiled kernel runs identically for every data point in a pattern, with only the offset advancing by a fixed stride, both on the CPU and the GPU. The trade-off is a larger COO array, proportional to the total number of pattern ×data-point contributions rather than to the number of unique structural nonzeros, which remains moderate for most applications. When a fully compressed format is needed, the conversion from COO to CSC carries a one-time cost for analyzing the sparsity structure. The repeated conversions that follow reuse a fixed structure, the typical case in nonlinear optimization, and are inexpensive. 

## 5.3 Callback Evaluation 

Each callback is implemented internally as a loop over patterns and data points, built from the expression trees, compressors, and offsets described above. The callback interface itself was introduced in Section 2.1. The zero-order callbacks NLPModels.obj and NLPModels.cons! are the simplest: the pattern’s tree is instantiated and evaluated at each data point. Objective terms are accumulated into the scalar objective value, and constraint values are written to their rows, located by the offset o0 .

5.3.1 Structure Callbacks 

The sparsity patterns themselves are exposed to the solver through the structure callbacks introduced in Section 2.1: 

34 1 2 1 2 1 2 · · · v: o1 o1step k = 1 k = 2 k = 3 cnt = 1 cnt = 2 cnt = 3 comp1 = (1, 2, 1) write position: o1 + (k-1)*o1step + comp1(cnt) 

Figure 5: Offset arithmetic for the sparse gradient of the Rosenbrock pattern, showing where each data point’s contributions are written in the global COO array. 

• NLPModels.jac structure! : fills the row and column index vectors i and j of the constraint Jacobian’s COO representation; 

• NLPModels.hess structure! : fills the corresponding index vectors of the Lagrangian Hessian. Both reuse the reverse-pass infrastructure of Section 5.1 once more. The passes run with NaN-backed sentinel sources (Section 5.2), and instead of accumulating numeric values, each variable leaf evaluates its variable index and stores it at the position determined by the Compressor (Section 5.2.2) and the offsets. The difference from the probe lies in what is instantiated: the probe is applied once per parameterized expression tree, whereas the structure evaluation is performed for each instantiation of the pattern. For data points 

k = 1 , . . . , N , the symbolic indices are resolved at the concrete data entry itr[k] . The resulting global row and column indices are written to the block [o1 + (k-1)*o1step + 1, ..., o1 + k*o1step] , and analogously with o2 and o2step for the Hessian. This loop runs over all data points of every pattern, but it is evaluated only once, at the beginning of the NLP solve. The numeric callbacks then refill only the value vector v at every iterate. 

5.3.2 Derivative Callbacks 

Everything described so far concerns a single algebraic pattern. A model holds several, stored as the obj and 

cons tuples of the ExaModel (Section 3.2). Each derivative callback applies the machinery of this section to each of them in turn, accumulating into the same global output array. What distinguishes one pattern’s contributions from another’s is where they are written, which is the role of the offsets carried by each 

SIMDFunction .The derivative callbacks first introduced in Section 2.1 are the dense gradient NLPModels.grad! , the sparse Jacobian NLPModels.jac coord! , and the sparse Hessian NLPModels.hess coord! . All three share the same mechanics. The reverse pass traverses the tree and counts the entry updates, incrementing a running counter 

cnt each time a leaf node is reached. The entry to update is offset + comp(cnt) , where offset locates the pattern’s block within the global COO array and comp(cnt) maps the counter to the correct slot among the pattern’s unique nonzeros. The offset advances by o1step (first order) or o2step (second order) for each data point, so data point k writes to positions [o1 + (k-1)*o1step + 1, ..., o1 + k*o1step] . Each data point has the same number of unique nonzeros, since that number is set by the pattern and not by the data. The offsets are therefore perfectly regular, and no dynamic bookkeeping is needed. Figure 5 illustrates this offset calculation for the gradient of the Rosenbrock pattern. 

• NLPModels.grad! (dense gradient): on the CPU, it runs the dense reverse pass drpass (Section 5.1), which accumulates each contribution ¯ v directly in place into the dense gradient vector. On the GPU, this direct accumulation would create race conditions, because threads evaluating different patterns may contribute to the same gradient entry at the same time. The GPU path therefore first evaluates a 

35 sparse but uncompressed gradient with grpass , each thread writing to its own conflict-free slot through the compressor and offsets. It then reduces that buffer into the dense gradient vector (Section 7.2.3). 

• NLPModels.jac coord! (sparse Jacobian): it loops over the collection of SIMDFunction s stored in the model (Section 3.4). For each pattern, the expression tree is instantiated at every data point and run through the sparse reverse pass jrpass (Section 5.1), which adds each contribution ¯ v to the COO value-vector entry v[offset + comp1(cnt)] . The Compressor (Section 5.2.2) merges the writes into the partially compressed form, and the offsets and strides stored in the SIMDFunction place each pattern’s block and advance the write position across data points. 

• NLPModels.hess coord! (sparse Hessian): it proceeds in the same way with the second-order passes 

hrpass and hdrpass (Section 5.1.3). Each diagonal and off-diagonal contribution is written into the COO value vector at the position determined by comp2 and the second-order offsets o2 and o2step 

(Section 3.4). 

# 6 GPU Kernel Generation 

ExaModels.jl evaluates models and their derivatives natively on the GPU. 

## 6.1 Selecting a GPU Backend 

Moving a model to the GPU takes one keyword argument at ExaCore construction. Nothing else in the model code changes:  

> 1

using ExaModels, CUDA, MadNLPGPU  

> 23

N = 10_000  

> 45

# CPU (default)  

> 6

core = ExaCore()  

> 78

# NVIDIA GPU: the only change on the user side  

> 9

core = ExaCore(backend = CUDABackend())  

> 10 11

# everything that follows remains the same  

> 12

@add_var(core, x, N; start = (mod(i,2)==1 ? -1.2 : 1.0 for i=1:N))  

> 13

@add_obj(core, 100(x[i]^2 - x[i+1])^2 + (x[i] - 1)^2 for i = 1:N-1)  

> 14

model = ExaModel(core)  

> 15

result = madnlp(model) # GPU-capable solver (MadNLPGPU.jl) 

The supported backends, provided through KernelAbstractions.jl (Churavy, 2025), are CUDA.jl (NVIDIA, 

CUDABackend() ), AMDGPU.jl (AMD, ROCBackend() ), oneAPI.jl (Intel), Metal.jl (Apple), and the CPU() 

backend for multithreaded execution on the host. The ExaCore then keeps all of its numerical arrays in the array type of the selected device. When the user supplies host arrays, for example as initial values or as parameter data, they are converted to that type as they are stored. Supplying the data in the device array type from the start avoids the conversion, but it is not required. The modeling syntax, the callbacks, and the solution access are the same as on the CPU. 

## 6.2 SIMDFunction as the Unit of Kernel Generation 

The SIMD abstraction of Section 2 expresses each pattern as a single function evaluated over a data iterator, and the parameterized expression tree of Section 4 is type-stable end to end, so the Julia compiler specializes a dedicated evaluator per pattern. The AD passes of Section 5 therefore map onto the device directly: each algebraic pattern becomes a GPU kernel, and each data point becomes a thread (Figure 6). All threads within a pattern execute the same compiled kernel and instantiate the same expression tree on different data 

36 expression tree (one pattern)  

> compiled kernel thread 1 thread 2 thread 3
> ...
> thread N
> v[o1+1 : o1+o1step] v[o1+o1step+1 : o1+2o1step] v[o1+2o1step+1 : o1+3o1step]
> ...
> v[o1+(N-1)o1step+1 : o1+No1step]
> itr[1] itr[2] itr[3] itr[N] one thread per data point disjoint output blocks (no race conditions)

Figure 6: Mapping of the SIMD abstraction onto a GPU. One kernel is compiled per algebraic pattern (left), one thread runs per data point (middle), and each thread writes to its own block of the output array (right). indices. The expression-tree structure therefore introduces no thread divergence, and every thread follows the same control flow, up to any branching inside the elementary functions themselves. This is well suited to GPU hardware. The implementation uses KernelAbstractions.jl for backend-agnostic kernels, written once with the @kernel macro and executed on any of the supported backends. The SIMDFunction of Section 3 is the unit of kernel generation. One kernel is compiled per pattern from its expression tree, and the Compressor mappings and offsets that the SIMDFunction carries tell each thread where to write. Within a pattern, the entries produced by data point k begin at o1 + ( k − 1) · o1step in the first-order output and at o2 + ( k − 1) · o2step in the second-order output. The strides o1step and o2step 

are the numbers of unique entries per data point, determined by the sparsity analysis of Section 5.2. A reverse-pass visit writes at that base plus the index returned by the corresponding compressor. Distinct data points therefore write to disjoint slices of the output arrays, and no two threads target the same position. The derivative kernels need no atomics and no locks. 

## 6.3 CPU vs. GPU Evaluation 

A GPU model exposes the same callback names as a CPU model, namely NLPModels.obj , NLPModels.cons! ,

NLPModels.grad! , NLPModels.jac coord! , and NLPModels.hess coord! . Julia’s multiple dispatch, keyed on the model’s backend and array types, selects the implementation. The two implementations of 

NLPModels.cons! follow, simplified to show the structure.  

> 1

# CPU (simplified)  

> 2

function NLPModels.cons!(  

> 3

> 4

for con in model.cons  

> 5

> 6

> 7

> 8

> 9

x, model. θ) 

> 10

end  

> 11

end  

> 12

end  

> 1

# GPU (simplified)  

> 2

function NLPModels.cons!(

model, x, g)  

> 4

> 5

> 6

g, con.f, con.itr,  

> 7

x, model. θ; 

> 8

ndrange =  

> 9

length(con.itr))  

> 10

end  

> 11

end 

> 12

Both implementations loop over the constraint patterns in the same way. They differ only in the inner iteration over data points. On the CPU, a @simd for loop evaluates the parameterized expression tree 

37 con.f(con.itr[i], x, model. θ) one data point at a time, and the annotation lets the compiler vectorize across data points using CPU SIMD instructions. On the GPU, the loop body becomes the body of the KernelAbstractions.jl kernel kerf , launched over the data iterator.  

> 1

# one thread per data point  

> 2

@kernel function kerf(y, @Const(f), @Const(itr),  

> 3

@Const(x), @Const( θ))  

> 4

I = @index(Global)  

> 5

@inbounds y[offset0(f, itr, I)] = f(itr[I], x, θ) 

> 6

end @index(Global) provides the thread’s global index I, and the launch with ndrange = length(con.itr) 

assigns one thread per data point. Each thread instantiates the expression tree at its own data point itr[I] 

and writes the result at offset0(f, itr, I) , the per-data-point base position described above, so distinct threads write to disjoint positions. Because f is a compile-time-specialized expression tree and a bits type (Section 4.2.3), it is a valid kernel argument. The kernel also contains no dynamic dispatch, which GPU compilation requires. Multithreaded execution on the host is obtained the same way, by passing CPU() as the backend. No separate implementation and no thread-specific specialization is written for it. KernelAbstractions.jl compiles the same kernel body for the host and, at launch, divides the index range evenly among the available Julia threads, each of which runs the kernel over its own contiguous block of data points (Churavy, 2025). The kernel is therefore data-parallel in the same sense as on a GPU, and only the number of threads differs. This is the backend used for the multithreaded CPU results of Section 8.3. The same comparison applies to the sparse gradient:  

> 1

# CPU (simplified):  

> 2

> 3

g, 1.0)

> 14

> 15

> 2

# sparse reverse pass  

> 3

function NLPModels.grad!(  

> 4

model, x, g)  

> 5

buf = model.ext.gradbuffer  

> 6

> 7

> 8

> 9

> 10

ndrange =  

> 11

> 12

> 13

> 14

end 

> 15

On the CPU, each data point’s forward evaluation builds the adjoint tree, and the dense reverse pass drpass 

(Section 5.1) accumulates contributions directly into the dense gradient vector. On the GPU, the wrapper launches the kernel kerg into a sparse buffer, which is then compressed into the dense gradient (Section 7.2.3).  

> 1

# the AD pass in each thread  

> 2

@kernel function kerg(y, @Const(f), @Const(itr),  

> 3

@Const(x), @Const( θ), @Const(adj))  

> 4

I = @index(Global)  

> 5

@inbounds grpass(f(itr[I], AdjointNodeSource(x), θ),  

> 6

f.comp1, y, offset1(f, I), 0, adj)  

> 7

end 

38 Each thread runs the full forward and reverse pass: the forward evaluation on an AdjointNodeSource builds the adjoint tree, and grpass writes the contributions to the thread’s own slot of the buffer, at positions determined by the Compressor f.comp1 and the base offset offset1(f, I) . The Jacobian and Hessian kernels follow the same structure, calling jrpass and hrpass0 respectively. The only thing that changes is the top-level wrapper that distributes the work. The pattern is instantiated over its data points, and each instantiation, that is, each forward and reverse pass, is mapped to a GPU thread. The expression trees are bits types, meaning concretely typed, immutable, and free of references to host memory (Section 4.2.3), so they are passed to the kernels directly. The partially compressed COO output format (Section 5.2) keeps the evaluation free of race conditions. The GPU is well utilized as long as the model carries many data points. Several further implementation choices, namely the native COO output format, the objective’s buffer-then-reduce evaluation, the gradient’s sparse-then-compress path, and the restriction to data-level parallelism, are described in Section 7.2.3. 

# 7 Implementation Notes 

This section collects implementation details deferred from the preceding sections. Each item records a design or engineering choice that matters for using or extending ExaModels.jl but is not needed to follow the main development. 

## 7.1 Model Construction 

7.1.1 Immutable ExaCore and Reconstruction 

The central design choice that enables type stability is that ExaCore is an immutable struct rather than a 

mutable struct . As described in Section 3.1, each call to @add var , @add obj , or @add con extends the component tuples and returns a new ExaCore . The type parameters of the new core encode the exact set of patterns added so far, so its concrete type differs from that of the previous core. The Julia compiler sees these as distinct concrete types and generates specialized code for each construction step. By the time 

ExaModel(core) is called, the final ExaCore already holds all expression trees, Compressor tuples, and offset computations. The instantiation builds no new expression trees or sparsity structures, only the solver-facing metadata and backend workspaces described in Section 3.2. This design contrasts with JuMP (Lubin, Dowson, et al., 2023) and similar AMSs, where the model object is a mutable container that accumulates variables, constraints, and objectives behind a single stable type. In JuMP, each @variable , @constraint , and @objective mutates internal dictionaries within the same Model 

object:  

> 1

model = Model(Ipopt.Optimizer)  

> 23

@variable(model, x[1:N]) # mutates model  

> 45

@constraint(model, [i = 1:N-2], g(x, i) ==0) # mutates model  

> 67

@objective(model, Min, sum(f(x, i) for i = 1:N-1)) # mutates model 

This API is natural and convenient, but the model’s concrete type does not change across these calls. All structure is hidden in runtime data structures. The derivative evaluation code must therefore dispatch dynamically on the stored expression types, which rules out the compile-time specialization described in Sections 4.2.3 and 6.3. The ExaModels.jl macros @add var , @add obj , and @add con are designed to look like the JuMP convention while achieving the opposite effect. Each macro call rebinds the name core to a new ExaCore of a different concrete type, so the compiler can track the growing model structure statically. The user writes: 

39 1 core = ExaCore(concrete = Val(true))  

> 2

# core :: ExaCore..., Tuple, Tuple  

> 34

@add_var(core, x, N; start = (x0(i) for i = 1:N))  

> 5

# core :: ExaCore..., TupleVariable, Tuple  

> 67

@add_con(core, g(x, i) for i = 1:N-2)  

> 8

@add_obj(core, f(x, i) for i = 1:N-1)  

> 9

# core :: ExaCore..., TupleVariable, TupleObjective..., ...  

> 10 11

model = ExaModel(core) 

This reads like a conventional modeling script, but each line produces a new concrete type that the compiler can fully specialize. The mutating appearance is achieved purely by macro expansion: @add var(core, x, n) unrolls to  

> 1

local _var  

> 2

core, _var = add_var(core, n; name = Val(:x))  

> 3

x = _var 

The underlying function add var returns a new core together with the variable handle. The macro rebinds the name core in the calling scope to the new object and binds the handle to x. No object is ever mutated. Creating a new ExaCore at every declaration may appear expensive, but the cost is negligible. The struct holds only counters, handles, and references to the numerical arrays, so each rebind copies the references while the arrays themselves are never recreated. For the example above with N = 10 6, the struct occupies 144 bytes when empty and 176, 304, and 392 bytes after the variable, constraint, and objective declarations. Each increment is the declared handle or wrapper stored inline in the core’s tuples. These figures were measured on a 64-bit machine with ExaModels.jl v0.11.2 and may change slightly across versions. The struct therefore grows with the number of declared blocks and patterns, not with the number of data points. It stays small for the typical large-scale model, which has few patterns. 

7.1.2 Reusable Expression Objects 

The @add expr macro defines a subexpression that can be reused in objectives and constraints. For example,  

> 1

@add_expr(core, s, x[i]^2 for i = 1:N)  

> 2

@add_obj(core, (s[i] - 1)^2 for i = 1:N)  

> 3

@add_con(core, s[i] + s[i+1] for i = 1:N-1; ucon = 1.0) 

The first line returns an Expression named s. It adds no objective, constraint, callback, or independent algebraic pattern to the model. Each later reference s[i] substitutes the corresponding expression tree directly into the objective or constraint that uses it. The object can therefore be reused at several sites without introducing auxiliary variables or constraint rows. An Expression {S,F,I,T } stores the information needed to reconstruct a reusable subexpression at a selected index: 

• size::S and length::Int : the shape and number of scalar entries; 

• f::F : the generator function that constructs the subexpression tree; 

• iter::I : the collected data iterator used when the expression is indexed; and 

• tag::T : optional user metadata. 

40 The add expr function returns an updated ExaCore together with the Expression . An anonymous expression is returned to the caller without being stored in the core. The named form @add expr(core, s, ...) also records the object in refs , making it accessible as core.s or model.s . This entry is only a name-to-object association. The expression is never added to the obj or cons tuples, is never compiled into its own 

SIMDFunction , and is never evaluated as a callback. Indexing it as s[i] instead invokes its generator for that index and substitutes the resulting expression tree into the objective or constraint being built. One consequence is that a subexpression referenced at several sites is re-evaluated at each site rather than computed once and shared. We have found this sufficient for the use cases of ExaModels.jl, since the subexpressions that arise in practice are inexpensive to re-evaluate. 

7.1.3 Compile-Time Constants and Algebraic Simplification 

The Constant {V} node embeds its value V as a type parameter rather than a struct field. This means the constant occupies zero runtime storage and is fully resolved at compile time. The Julia compiler (and the AOT compiler juliac --trim=safe ) can propagate the value concretely through all downstream operations. This design also enables algebraic simplification at model-construction time. Trivial branches are collapsed before the expression tree is finalized. The identities applied automatically include the following: 

x + 0 → x, x · 1 → x, x/ 1 → x, x0 → 1, x1 → x,

x2 → abs2( x), x−1 → inv( x), 0 · x → 0, 0/x → 0. These rules reduce the depth of the expression tree and remove unnecessary operations. This matters most for GPU kernels, where every instruction counts. The simplification is exact, with no floating-point approximation, because it operates on the symbolic tree structure rather than on numeric values. Integer literal exponents reach the power rules through a lowering step in Julia rather than through the registered operators of Section 7.1.4. Such an exponent is lowered by the compiler to Base.literal pow ,with the exponent carried as a Val type parameter. The expression x[i]^2 therefore arrives at ExaModels.jl as literal pow(^, x[i], Val {2}) rather than as a call to ^ with the runtime value 2. The exponent is thus known to the method that builds the node, which is what allows x2 to be recorded as an abs2 node and 

x1 to be replaced by x. The derivative rules of abs2 are then used in place of the general power rule, and no exponent is stored or dispatched on at evaluation time. The other rules in the table fire when an operand is a 

Constant node, as in x−1 → inv( x). 

7.1.4 Function Registration 

For a function to appear in an ExaModels.jl expression, it must be registered . The system needs the function itself together with its first and second derivatives, which are required for the gradient, Jacobian, and Lagrangian Hessian computations of Section 5. ExaModels.jl provides two macros for this purpose: 

• @register univariate(f, df, ddf) : registers a unary function with its first and second derivatives. 

• @register bivariate(f, df1, df2, ddf11, ddf12, ddf22) : registers a binary function with its partial derivatives up to second order. Each macro generates the following method groups via multiple dispatch: 

• a primal method that constructs a Node1 or Node2 when applied to AbstractNode arguments; 

• a constant-folding method for Constant arguments; 

• a first-order adjoint method that constructs an AdjointNode1 or AdjointNode2 with the derivative values; 

• a second-order adjoint method that constructs a SecondAdjointNode1 or SecondAdjointNode2 ; and 

• an evaluation method that applies the function when the constructed node is called on a data point. 

41 ExaModels.jl v0.11.2 ships with 61 pre-registered function signatures (52 univariate and 9 bivariate), which cover the operations needed by most algebraic models: 

• arithmetic: +, − (unary and binary), ∗, /, and ^ (power); 

• exponential and logarithmic: exp , exp2 , exp10 , expm1 , log , log2 , log10 , log1p , sqrt , cbrt ;

• trigonometric: sin , cos , tan , csc , sec , cot ; the inverses asin , acos , atan (including the two-argument form), acot ; the degree variants sind , cosd , tand , cscd , secd , cotd , atand , acotd ; and sinpi , cospi ,

sinc ;

• hyperbolic: sinh , cosh , tanh , csch , sech , coth , and the inverses asinh , acosh , atanh , acoth ;

• utility: abs , abs2 , inv , sign , signbit , floor , ceil , max , min , hypot , deg2rad , rad2deg .Each signature is registered together with its first and second derivatives. Less common functions are provided through extension packages, so that the dependency is incurred only when needed. The ExaModelsSpecialFunctions extension is loaded when SpecialFunctions.jl is present. It registers erf , erfc , erfinv , gamma , digamma , trigamma , beta , the Bessel functions besselj0 , besselj1 ,

bessely0 , and bessely1 , and the Airy functions airyai and airybi , among others, all with the derivative rules required for AD. Beyond these, users can register custom functions with the same two macros. The registered derivatives enter the type system through multiple dispatch, so no modification of ExaModels.jl internals is required. 

## 7.2 Callback Implementation 

7.2.1 Compiler Specialization of the Callbacks 

The expression tree type is fully concrete and known at compile time (Section 4.2.3), and the AD traversal is annotated with @inline to force aggressive inlining. The Julia compiler therefore produces specialized machine code for each pattern with no interpretive overhead. Forcing inlining in this way is also what makes AOT compilation possible (Section 7.3.1). The generated code for a complete gradient kernel is reproduced in Section A. As detailed there, the kernel uses 13 floating-point operations against the best hand-written sequence of 12 that we could construct, and no unnecessary overhead is observable in the LLVM code. Each user-facing callback, namely the objective, constraint, gradient, Jacobian, and Hessian evaluations, thus compiles to a single piece of LLVM code, compiled once per model. This lets the compiler optimize the entire evaluation as a whole. All callbacks are also non-allocating on the CPU, so no garbage-collection pauses occur during the solve. The user writes natural mathematical expressions, dispatch builds the tree, and the compiler produces code whose performance is competitive with hand-written source transformations. 

7.2.2 Affine-Node Specialization 

Many expression trees contain long chains of affine operations (+, −, multiplication by a constant) whose second derivatives are identically zero. When a binary operation combines one subtree with a plain scalar, the adjoint construction reduces it to a unary node whose function is recorded as FirstFixed {F} or 

SecondFixed {F}. These wrapper types indicate that the first or the second argument of the binary function 

F is the fixed scalar. ExaModels.jl exploits this in the second-order reverse pass of Section 5 with hrpass0 , a specialized entry point that dispatches on the function type. For affine SecondAdjointNode1 nodes, such as FirstFixed {typeof(*) } and SecondFixed {typeof(+) }, the h term is known to be zero at compile time. The recursion then propagates only (¯ v · y, ¯v2 · y2) and never computes the ¯ v · h contribution. This removes unnecessary multiplications and additions from the inner loop, in particular for the common case of weighted sums of nonlinear terms. The specialization is selected by dispatch on the type of the expression tree, so the choice is resolved at compile time and no branch on the affine case remains in the generated code. 

42 7.2.3 GPU Evaluation Details Jacobian and Hessian evaluation with partial compression only. For Jacobian and Hessian evaluation, each data point in a pattern writes to its own contiguous block of the COO value array (Section 5.2). Different data points write to disjoint positions, so all writes are conflict-free and no atomics or synchronization are needed. This follows from the SIMD structure. Within a pattern, data point k has first-order block base offset o1 + ( k−1) · o1step , and a reverse-pass visit writes at that base plus the one-based index returned by 

comp1 . The second-order path uses o2 , o2step , and comp2 in the same way. ExaModels.jl therefore stores Jacobian and Hessian entries in the partially compressed COO format of Section 5.2 and does not further compress them to CSC format. Full compression would require sorting and merging entries. Avoiding it also suits solvers such as Ipopt (W¨ achter and Biegler, 2006) and MadNLP.jl (Shin, Pacaud, and Montoison, 2025), which accept COO input directly. 

Objective evaluation by buffering and reduction. The objective callback evaluates f (x) = 

P

> ℓ

PIℓ 

> i=1

˜f (ℓ)(pi, x, θ ). On the CPU, this sum is computed by direct scalar accumulation while iterating over patterns and data points (Section 6.3). No array of objective terms is formed, so the CPU objective path does not use o0 . On the GPU, direct accumulation by many threads would require atomics, so ExaModels.jl first evaluates the objective terms into a buffer of length P 

> ℓ

Iℓ. Each pattern’s o0 identifies the beginning of its disjoint slice of this buffer, and thread i writes its value at o0 + i . Once all pattern kernels have completed, a parallel sum reduction over the buffer produces the scalar objective value. Thus, unlike the CPU objective path, the GPU implementation requires the zero-order offset to place contributions before the subsequent reduction. 

Gradient evaluation by sparse pass and compression. On the CPU, the gradient is computed by the dense reverse pass drpass (Section 5.1), which accumulates each contribution ¯ v directly into the dense gradient vector. On the GPU, the sparse reverse pass grpass is used instead. Each thread writes its gradient contributions into a sparse buffer indexed by variable index and position, and a subsequent KernelAbstractions.jl kernel compress to dense reduces that buffer into the dense gradient vector. Two phases are needed because threads from different patterns may contribute to the same gradient entry, and atomic additions to a dense vector would cause contention:  

> 1

@kernel function compress_to_dense(  

> 2

y, @Const(y0), @Const(ptr), @Const(sparsity))  

> 3

I = @index(Global)  

> 4

@inbounds for j = ptr[I]:(ptr[I+1]-1)  

> 5

(k, l) = sparsity[j]  

> 6

y[k] +=y0[l]  

> 7

end  

> 8

end 

The ptr array partitions the sparse buffer entries by target variable, so each thread of the compression kernel handles a distinct set of target indices without conflicts. This partition, together with the sorted sparsity array, is computed once at ExaModel instantiation (Section 3.2). The evaluation of ConstraintAugmentation s uses the same strategy. Several augmentations may contribute to the same constraint entry, so their contributions are written into a separate buffer and reduced into the constraint vector by the same compress to dense 

kernel. 

Parallelism over data points only. ExaModels.jl parallelizes at the data-point level, so each thread executes the full forward–reverse AD pass for one data point of one pattern. The expression tree itself is never split across threads. This design keeps the kernel simple and avoids the synchronization overhead that splitting the tree traversal would incur. For typical NLP patterns, the expression trees are small, on the order of tens of nodes, so the parallelism from thousands of data points is more than enough to saturate GPU hardware. The result is high occupancy with minimal launch overhead, at one kernel launch per pattern per callback. 

43 7.3 Compile-Time Design 

7.3.1 Type Stability and Ahead-of-Time Compilation 

A Julia function is type-stable if the compiler can infer the concrete type of every variable and return value from the types of the arguments alone, with no runtime dispatch. GPU kernels require it, since dynamic dispatch is not supported on GPU hardware, and Julia’s AOT compiler juliac --trim=safe relies on it to produce standalone binaries with unused code eliminated. Type stability originates in the construction design of Section 3. The ExaCore is immutable, and each macro call produces a core of a new concrete type whose parameters encode the model built so far. The final type therefore determines the model structure completely, and every function invoked on the model is compiled specifically for that type. The construction-level details, including the contrast with mutable model containers such as JuMP’s, are given in Section 7.1.1. Because all ExaModels.jl code paths are type-stable, the entire model construction and solve pipeline is compatible with juliac --trim=safe . The compiler produces a standalone binary or shared library that runs without a Julia installation, since the required runtime libraries are bundled with the artifact. Combined with NLPModelsIpoptLite.jl, a lightweight AOT-compatible wrapper around the Ipopt C library, a complete optimization application can be packaged as a single executable:  

> 1

# RosenbrockApp.jl: compiles to a binary  

> 2

module RosenbrockApp  

> 3

using ExaModels, NLPModelsIpoptLite  

> 4

function (@main)(ARGS)  

> 5

N = parse(Int, ARGS[1])  

> 67

core = ExaCore(concrete = Val(true))  

> 8

@add_var(core, x, N; start = (mod(i,2)==1 ? -1.2 : 1.0 for i=1:N))  

> 9

@add_obj(core, 100(x[i]^2 - x[i+1])^2 + (x[i] - 1)^2 for i = 1:N-1)  

> 10 11

model = ExaModel(core)  

> 12

result = ipopt(model; print_level = 5)  

> 13

return result.status ==0 ? 0 : 1  

> 14

end  

> 15

end 

Running juliac --trim=safe --output-exe rosenbrock RosenbrockApp.jl produces a standalone bi-nary that solves the Rosenbrock problem for any N specified at the command line, with no JIT compilation latency. As of this writing, AOT compilation is supported for CPU backends only. GPU backends still require Julia’s JIT runtime for kernel compilation. A model, or an entire model library, developed with ExaModels.jl can be compiled together with a solver into a standalone binary, or into a shared library whose entry points are exposed to other programs. Developers can therefore build model libraries on ExaModels.jl without imposing a Julia-runtime dependence on their users. The library ships as a conventional compiled artifact that can be called from Python, C++, or any language with a C foreign-function interface, which eases deployment in production environments. 

7.3.2 Generic Numeric Precision 

Julia’s multiple dispatch and JIT compilation make the entire ExaModels.jl pipeline generic over the floating-point type. This covers expression tree construction, AD evaluation, and the solver callbacks. All internal code operates on the abstract type T <: AbstractFloat , and the compiler specializes automatically:  

> 1

core32 = ExaCore(Float32) # all arrays use Float32  

> 2

core64 = ExaCore(Float64) # all arrays use Float64 

44 This extends to user-defined numeric types, including simulated higher precisions. One example is the double-double type Double64 of DoubleFloats.jl (Sarnoff and JuliaMath, 2022), which simulates roughly quadruple precision with a pair of Float64 s. Another is Julia’s built-in arbitrary-precision BigFloat . Both run through the entire pipeline with no code changes beyond specifying the type at ExaCore construction. The Constant {V} type parameter (Section 7.1.3) ensures that compile-time constants are converted to the appropriate precision by replace T , so no mixed-precision artifacts arise. 

Float32 arithmetic is much faster than Float64 on consumer GPUs, and some hardware does not support double precision at all, not even through software emulation. Apple GPUs, targeted by the Metal backend, execute only single-precision arithmetic, and ExaModels.jl models run there entirely in Float32 . The same genericity also serves applications that require extended precision, such as sensitivity analysis near degeneracy. 

Implications for model libraries. Portability also propagates to model libraries built on top of ExaModels.jl, such as ExaModelsPower.jl and COPSBenchmark.jl. Such a library need only accept the numeric type and the backend as parameters, pass them to ExaCore(T; backend = backend, concrete = Val(true)) , convert its numerical data to T, and avoid type-unstable constructs. It then inherits arbitrary precision, every GPU backend, and AOT compatibility without further effort:  

> 1

function my_model(data; T = Float64, backend = nothing)  

> 2

core = ExaCore(T; backend = backend, concrete = Val(true))  

> 34

@add_var(core, x, length(data))  

> 5

@add_obj(core, f(x, d) for d in convert.(T, data))  

> 67

return ExaModel(core)  

> 8

end 

## 7.4 Two-Stage and Batch Structures 

The extensively studied classes of structured NLPs include two-stage stochastic programs (Birge and Louveaux, 1997; Lubin, Petra, et al., 2011; Chiang, Petra, and Zavala, 2014), graph-structured problems (Jalving, Shin, and Zavala, 2022; Cole and Zavala, 2024), and batch optimization (Amos and Kolter, 2017; Kim et al., 2025). Of these, the two-stage and batch structures are the ones aligned with the SIMD abstraction. Each adds an axis of repetition over which the same patterns are evaluated, so capturing it at the modeling level extends SIMD parallelism to the scenario and instance axes. Graph-induced structure is less amenable, since an irregular graph yields heterogeneous algebraic patterns. ExaModels.jl accordingly targets both structures within a single formulation. No separate data structures are needed, since every problem can be regarded as a batched two-stage problem. A standard NLP is the special case of one batch instance and no recourse variables. A further mechanism, the external oracle, accommodates components that cannot be expressed as parameterized expression trees. It is described in Section 7.5. 

7.4.1 Unified Formulation 

The most general formulation targeted by ExaModels.jl is a batched two-stage parametric NLP. Given B ≥ 1independent batch instances and S ≥ 0 scenarios per instance, the problem is min 

> {xb,s }
> B

X

> b=1

f0(xb, 0; θb, 0) + 

> S

X

> s=1

fs(xb, 0, x b,s ; θb,s )

!

s.t. g0(xb, 0; θb, 0) ∈ C 0, b = 1 , . . . , B, gs(xb, 0, x b,s ; θb,s ) ∈ C s, s = 1 , . . . , S, b = 1 , . . . , B, 

(5) where the scenario index s runs over 0 , 1, . . . , S and s = 0 denotes the root. The vectors xb, 0 are the root, or first-stage, variables of instance b, and xb,s for s ≥ 1 are the scenario, or second-stage, variables of that 

45 instance. The vectors θb,s are parameters, which are not optimized over. Each fs(·) and gs(·) has the SIMD structure of (3), with scenario probabilities absorbed into fs(·). Formulation (5) has two structural dimensions. The first is the scenarios, over which the root variables are shared while the scenario variables and constraints are replicated. The second is the batch instances, which share the same expression trees, and hence the same compiled functions, but differ in their numerical data. The instances couple through neither the objective nor the constraints, so minimizing the aggregated objective is equivalent to solving the B instances independently. The batched form exists so that all instances are evaluated by one set of kernels. Both dimensions are optional and can be active at the same time: B = 1 gives a two-stage stochastic program, S = 0 gives a batch of standard NLPs, and S = 0 with B = 1 recovers (3). All scenarios and batch instances share the same algebraic patterns. The SIMDFunction s, Compressor maps, and sparsity structures are therefore built once and reused across every scenario and instance. Their concrete types coincide, so the functions specialized for them are compiled only once. The only quantities that vary are numerical: parameter values, variable and constraint bounds, and the primal and dual initial points. This is what makes the extensions efficient. It is also why ExaModels.jl provides accessors that address a single scenario, modifying those arrays without touching the model structure and hence without triggering recompilation. The goal of ExaModels.jl is not to provide decomposition algorithms, which are the domain of algorithm and software developers. It is to provide what such algorithms need, namely efficient callbacks for structured models together with the structural information required to exploit them. ExaModels.jl therefore tags each variable and constraint with the stage it belongs to, and reports the scenario that each variable and constraint belongs to as well as the number of scenarios. With these, a developer can implement methods such as Benders decomposition or progressive hedging on top of the model. 

7.4.2 Two-Stage Stochastic Programs 

The two-stage structure is declared with TwoStageExaCore(nscen) together with a marker EachScenario() 

that distinguishes root from scenario declarations, as in the following example:  

> 1

S = 4 # number of scenarios  

> 2

core = TwoStageExaCore(S)  

> 34

@add_var(core, x, 2) # root (shared)  

> 5

@add_var(core, z, EachScenario(), 3) # per scenario  

> 6

@add_par(core, theta, EachScenario(), theta_vals)  

> 78

@add_obj(core, x[1]^2 + x[2]^2) # root objective  

> 9

@add_obj(core, (z[j, s] - theta[j, s])^2 for (j, s) in idx)  

> 10

@add_con(core, EachScenario(), (z[j, s] + x[2] for (j, s) in idx))  

> 11 12

model = ExaModel(core) 

where theta vals holds the per-scenario parameter values and idx collects the index pairs ( j, s ) over which the scenario patterns are evaluated. The marker also applies to @add par and @add con , and appends the scenario index as the last dimension of the declared block. Objectives are declared with the plain @add obj ,with the scenario structure carried by the data iterator. Internally, the scenario blocks are replicated S times and tagged by stage. The scenario patterns are therefore still constructed once and evaluated across all scenarios in parallel, with the scenario index acting as an additional data dimension. Two-stage models also provide counterparts of the model data accessors of Section 2.2 that address a single scenario. Parameter values, bounds, and initial points can thus be set and queried one scenario at a time, again without touching the model structure or the compiled functions. Their signatures are given in the online documentation (Shin and contributors, 2025). 

46 7.4.3 Batch Models 

The batch structure solves B independent instances that share the same expression trees, and hence the same compiled functions, but differ in parameter values. All instances are evaluated together, with each pair of a data point and an instance mapped to a separate thread. This adds a second level of parallelism on top of the parallelism across data points, and it is useful for parametric sweeps, ensemble optimization, and warm-starting. At the time of writing, this extension is under active development in the ExaModels.jl repository and is not part of a released version. It follows the scenario mechanism of Section 7.4.2: declarations are replicated across instances, the internal storage generalizes from vectors to matrices, and a flattening wrapper exposes the batch model as a standard vector-valued NLPModels.AbstractNLPModel .

## 7.5 External Oracles 

ExaModels.jl does not aim to provide efficient callbacks for every class of function. Components such as neural-network layers, PDE solvers, and custom GPU kernels are handled more efficiently by external libraries that already supply derivatives. For these cases, ExaModels.jl provides an external oracle interface. Let x be a variable block of length nx and let f (·) : Rnx → Rm be an opaque map. The call embed oracle(core, x, m; ...) adds a block z of m auxiliary output variables, registers z − f (x) = 0 as an oracle constraint, and returns the updated core, the block z, and the oracle object. The user supplies matrix-free callbacks that operate on local vectors, without global offsets: f! (function evaluation y = f (x)), jvp! (Jacobian–vector product Jf (x) v), vjp! (transposed-Jacobian–vector product Jf (x)⊤w), and an optional hvp! (Hessian–vector product ( Pmi=1 wi∇2fi(x)) v).  

> 1

core, z, _ = embed_oracle(  

> 2

core, x, N;  

> 3

f! = (y, xv) ->(y .= xv .^ 2; nothing),  

> 4

jvp! = (Jv, xv, v) ->(Jv .= 2 .* xv .* v; nothing),  

> 5

vjp! = (Jtv, xv, w)-> (Jtv .= 2 .* xv .* w; nothing),  

> 6

hvp! = (Hv, xv, w, v) ->(Hv .= 2 .* w .* v; nothing),  

> 7

) 

> 8

@add_obj(core, z[i] + sin(x[i]) for i in 1:N) 

The oracle output z is a regular Variable and can be combined with SIMD expressions in any subsequent objective or constraint. 

# 8 Numerical Experiments 

This section validates the preceding claims numerically. It measures the speedup those callbacks obtain on a GPU, and shows their evaluation time to be O(1) in the number of data points, as claimed earlier, for as long as the device is not saturated. It then exercises the callbacks inside a GPU-resident optimization solver, to quantify what that speedup is worth in a complete solve. 

## 8.1 Benchmark Problems 

We evaluate ExaModels.jl on three benchmark suites that cover a range of problem structures and scales: the Lukˇ san–Vlˇ cek collection, the COPS test set, and PGLIB-OPF instances. 

Lukˇ san–Vlˇ cek problems. The Lukˇ san–Vlˇ cek collection (Lukˇ san and Vlˇ cek, 1999) consists of 18 scalable, sparse, equality-constrained NLPs. They were designed for testing unconstrained and equality-constrained optimization algorithms. Most are synthetic instances with no direct application context, but their partially separable structure makes them useful for benchmarking AD systems. Each problem is parameterized by the number of variables N . The objectives and constraints are sums of partially separable functions, which fits the SIMD abstraction directly. The collection includes generalized Rosenbrock, Wood, Cragg–Levy, Powell, 

47 Broyden (tridiagonal and banded), and chained variants of the Hock–Schittkowski problems (HS46–HS53), among others. Implementations for ExaModels.jl are provided in LuksanVlcekBenchmark.jl (MadSuite, 2026). 

COPS problems. The COPS test set (Dolan and Mor´ e, 2001) is a collection of 18 scalable NLPs from applications in optimal control, fluid dynamics, parameter estimation, PDE-constrained optimization, and mesh generation. The problems include, among others, the Goddard rocket, robot arm, glider trajectory, journal bearing, minimum surface, torsion, catenary chain, and several chemical engineering models (gas–oil, methanol, pinene, catalytic mixing). Each problem is parameterized by a sizing parameter, which for the PDE-based problems is a discretization resolution. The suite spans a wide range of problem structures: some problems are unconstrained, others have equality or inequality constraints, and several involve nonlinear PDEs discretized on 2D grids. Implementations for ExaModels.jl are provided in COPSBenchmark.jl (MadSuite, 2024). 

PGLIB-OPF problems. The PGLib-OPF benchmark library (Babaeinejadsarookolaee et al., 2021) provides standardized ACOPF instances ranging from 3 to 78,484 buses. These formulations, and the case data format the library distributes them in, have a lineage: MATPOWER (Zimmerman, Murillo-Sanchez, and Thomas, 2011) established the reference implementations and the case format that the field still uses, and PowerModels.jl (Coffrin et al., 2018) later expressed the polar, rectangular, and relaxed power flow formulations over that format in a common framework. We test all 66 instances in the polar (ACP) voltage formulation using ExaModelsPower.jl (Johnson et al., 2025), which provides ExaModels implementations in that tradition. Every instance has the same algebraic structure of 15 algebraic patterns. The instances differ only in the network data, that is, the bus parameters, the line admittances, the power demands, and the number of data points over which each pattern is repeated. ExaModels.jl therefore compiles the expression trees and the AD kernels once for the whole set of 66 instances. The first instance triggers compilation, and every later one reuses the compiled functions and changes only the data arrays. The pattern–data separation of Section 2.2 thus applies at the scale of a benchmark library. The OPF problems also carry inequality constraints and variable bounds, which the LV problems do not. The OPF instances are parameterized by the network topology rather than a scalar sizing parameter, making them complementary to the scalable LV and COPS problems. 

Exclusion of CUTEst. CUTEst (Gould, Orban, and Toint, 2015) is the standard test environment for mathematical optimization. We do not use it for three reasons. First, most instances in the collection are small enough that data-parallel evaluation has nothing to work with, so GPU acceleration is neither necessary nor useful for them. Second, those instances that are scalable are each parameterized by their own set of admissible dimensions rather than by a common scalable size, so the collection does not offer a size sweep of the kind our experiments require. Third, the large CUTEst instances are drawn largely from the same families as the LV and COPS suites, which we benchmark directly. 

Instance sizes. For each LV and COPS problem, we select three values of the sizing parameter targeting approximately 10 2, 10 4, and 10 6 Hessian nonzeros (nnzh), producing small , medium , and large instances. The relation between the sizing parameter and nnzh varies across problems, being linear for chain, quadratic for bearing, and cubic for polygon, so the sizes are chosen per problem to reach the target ranges. For the LV suite, we use N ∈ { 20 , 2,000 , 200 ,000 } across all 18 problems. For COPS, the sizes are problem-specific: for example, bearing uses grid sizes (5 × 5), (35 × 35), and (320 × 320), while catmix uses N ∈ { 3, 200 , 20 ,000 }.The PGLIB-OPF instances span a natural range from 3 to 78,484 buses. In total, the benchmark comprises 18 × 3 = 54 LV instances, 18 × 3 = 54 COPS instances, and 66 OPF instances, for 174 benchmark instances.

Reproducibility. The benchmark implementation, the generators that produce every table and fig-ure in this section, and the exact package environment are available at https://github.com/exanauts/ exa-models-benchmark . The batch scripts that ran each leg of the campaign are there too, in slurm/ and named by site as orcd-* and jlse-* , alongside a Makefile whose targets drive the runs, fetch the archived
