Each run is archived on the results branch of that repository as a self-contained bundle, one directory per run, holding the benchmarking script, the hardware description of the machine, the raw per-instance timings in machine-readable form, and the resolved Manifest.toml . The hardware used for benchmarking is listed in Table 2, where each platform is named by a label whose letter gives the vendor: C is CPU only, N NVIDIA, A AMD, and M Apple. The AMD and B200 systems are provided by the Joint Laboratory for System Evaluation at Argonne National Laboratory, and all other clusters are part of MIT’s Office of Research Computing and Data. The manifest fixes the software environment of that run, recording the Julia version and the resolved version of every package in the dependency graph, including the commit of ExaModels.jl used to generate the results, KernelAbstractions.jl, CUDA.jl, and the CUDA runtime and driver artifacts. The results reported here were produced with Julia 1.12 and ExaModels.jl v0.11.2 (Shin and contributors, 2025), in Float64 arithmetic unless noted. Every number reported in this section can therefore be traced to the run that produced it and to the environment in which it ran. 

Compilation. Julia compiles the model-construction code and the evaluation callbacks the first time a given model structure is encountered. That first-time cost is substantial, and it is specific to the problem, since a new set of algebraic patterns requires a new set of compiled functions. The concern of this paper is runtime performance, so every timing reported in this section is a steady-state measurement and excludes that compilation. Model creation and compilation are measured jointly in Section 8.6. This holds for the model creation times as well: they measure steady-state construction, not the first call that triggers compilation. 

Aggregate metric. We report the shifted geometric mean (SGM) with a shift of 10 μs as the aggregate performance metric. Denoting the wall times of a modeling system on problems i = 1 , . . . , n by ti, the SGM is SGM( t; σ) = exp 

 1

n

> n

X

> i=1

ln( ti + σ)



− σ, 

where the shift is σ = 10 −5 s. The SGM is less sensitive to outliers than the arithmetic mean and is standard in optimization benchmarking. Instances are classified into the Small , Medium , and Large size classes by nnz = max(nnzj , nnzh). Full per-instance timings are provided in Section B. 

GPU timing protocol. A GPU callback carries two costs that a CPU callback does not. The first is the kernel launch cost, a few microseconds per call, which a solver pays as well and which every timing we report includes. The second is host–device synchronization, paid only when the host reads a device value. A device-resident solver does not do that after each callback, since the results stay in device arrays that the following linear algebra consumes, so this cost is purely an artifact of measuring from the host rather than a property of the callback. We exclude it not by measuring and subtracting it, but by making it negligible. The timing routine of the benchmarking script is, in simplified form:  

> 1

f(); sync() # warm up  

> 2

sync() # quiesce the device  

> 3

t0 = time_ns() 

49 4 for i = 1:N # N back-to-back launches  

> 5

f()  

> 6

end  

> 7

sync() # wait for the last kernel  

> 8

(time_ns() - t0) / 1e9 / N # apparent per-call time 

The batch size N is calibrated from three synchronized single calls so that the batch spans 0 .5 s, and the reported value is the best of three batches. The apparent per-call time satisfies ˆt = N t + tsync 

N = t + tsync 

N ,

where ˆ t is the reported estimate of the average callback time, t is the true average per-call cost, tsync is the one synchronization inside the timed interval, and N is the batch size. The second term is the bias. It decreases as 1 /N and is determined by the batch duration N t rather than by the speed of the individual kernel. A 0 .5 s batch against a synchronization of order 10 μs therefore yields a relative bias of approximately 2 × 10 −5

for every callback, irrespective of its speed. The bias is positive, so the reported time overestimates the GPU callback time. 

## 8.3 NLP Function Evaluation 

We measure the NLP function evaluation performance of ExaModels.jl, on the CPU and on a range of GPU platforms. The callbacks are NLPModels.obj , NLPModels.cons! , NLPModels.grad! , NLPModels.jac coord! ,and NLPModels.hess coord! . Every timing is the minimum wall time over repeated trials, which is robust to system noise. Speedups are reported against single-threaded execution, the standard sequential baseline. The multithreaded row of each summary table shows the best thread count among 4, 8, and 16; the full ladder appears in the appendix tables, and the summary row’s choice reflects where adding threads stops helping. Each summary table reports one NVIDIA GPU, the one with the highest aggregate speedup on the large size class, together with the AMD and Intel GPUs. The composite row of each summary table aggregates the five callbacks into one evaluation cost per solve. The composite approximates the time a modeling system spends on NLP function evaluation when it is deployed inside an optimization solver. Each instance’s callback times are weighted by the call counts of a GPU-resident solve of the largest optimal power flow (OPF) instance, 115 objective, 115 constraint, 114 gradient, 114 Jacobian, and 113 Hessian evaluations, and the row reports the SGM of the weighted sum over the instances of the class. The weights are derived from that one OPF solve and reused across all three suites, so on the LV and COPS suites the row is a weighting of measured callback times, not a measurement of a solve. The full per-instance results appear in Section B, and Tables 3 to 5 summarize them as SGMs, one table per suite. ExaModels.jl compiles a derivative evaluation function specialized to the type of each expression tree, and pays that compilation cost upfront, once per model structure (Section 8.6). Section A shows the compiled gradient kernel that ExaModels.jl produces, in which almost nothing remains beyond the arithmetic the pattern requires. The GPU rows of the same summary tables run the same callbacks on the same instances under the timing protocol of Section 8.2. The GPU speedups grow with problem size across the three suites, reaching 76 × for the Hessian on the large size class of the LV suite and 30 × on the large size class of the COPS suite. On small instances the GPU is slower than the CPU, and on medium instances the two are within a factor of two. The growth of the speedup reflects GPU times that are nearly constant in the problem size rather than GPU performance that improves with size. On the LV suite, the Hessian takes 26 .9, 28 .3, and 35 .7 μs on the small, medium, and large size classes, and the Jacobian 19 .2, 19 .9, and 20 .4 μs, while the CPU times grow by more than three orders of magnitude across the same classes.

The absolute level of the GPU times, a few tens of microseconds, is set by the kernel launch overhead. The flatness across four orders of magnitude in problem size is the constant-time parallel evaluation argued in Section 1, realized in practice for as long as the device is not saturated. The spread across devices is modest. On the large-instance LV Hessian, the five NVIDIA cards measured span 34 .8–76 .4×, the AMD Instinct MI300X reaches 44 .5×, the Intel Intel Data Center GPU Max 1550 reaches 27 .4×, and the Apple M2 Pro reaches 23 .3×. The COPS suite shows the same modest spread. The same kernels run across four vendors without change. The Apple timings, in the per-instance figures and the appendix tables, are in Float32 , while all other series are in Float64 .The LV problems show larger GPU speedups than the COPS problems. The absolute Hessian times show the same thing, 92 .9 μs on large COPS instances against 35 .7 μs on large LV instances. On the PGLIB-OPF instances the acceleration is more moderate still, reaching 7 .3× for the Hessian on the large size class, and the objective and gradient callbacks show no speedup there. The LV expression trees are simpler and more uniform, which gives a higher arithmetic intensity per data point. The COPS problems have more expression structures, as in the rocket dynamics with its exponentials and divisions, and this raises register pressure and lowers GPU occupancy. The OPF objective is a simple sum over generators and offers little parallelism, which is why the objective and gradient callbacks do not accelerate there. The multi-threaded CPU rows use the KernelAbstractions.jl CPU() backend, so they run the same data-parallel kernels as the GPU rows, one thread per data point, on host threads (Section 6.3). On the large instances of the LV and COPS suites they stay an order of magnitude below the GPU on the same callbacks.

## 8.4 Per-Instance GPU Scaling 

The class-level summaries above aggregate over instances; this subsection follows single instances as they grow, on the CPU and CUDA backends of platform N6. The first ladder is the Lukˇ san–Vlˇ cek Rosenbrock problem at N = 20, 2 ,000, and 200 ,000; the second is the ACOPF polar formulation at case118 ieee ,

case1354 pegase , and case78484 epigrids .The ExaModels.jl GPU Hessian takes 16.2 μs, 55.9 μs, and 119.3 μs over the Rosenbrock ladder, and the Jacobian takes 9.6 μs, 20.8 μs, and 41.6 μs. The times grow with N , while the problem grows by four orders of magnitude. The growth shows the saturation boundary of the constant-time argument of Section 8.3: this lad-der runs on the RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell of platform N6, whose reported FP64 peak is 1 .9 TFLOPS (NVIDIA Corporation, 2025), against 40 TFLOPS for the B200 of the summary tables (NVIDIA Corporation, 2024), where the Hessian stays within 26.9–35.7 μs across the size classes. The launch cost makes the CPU the faster device at N = 20, and the objective callback, a single scalar reduction, gains little from the GPU at any size. A callback issues one kernel per algebraic pattern it touches, and one more to zero its output buffer. The polar formulation has one objective pattern and fourteen constraint patterns. The Hessian touches all fifteen patterns, so NLPModels.hess coord! issues sixteen kernels. The objective does not enter the Jacobian,

AMDGPU (A1) 103.89 ms (0 .0×) 101.24 ms (0 .1×) 102.03 ms (2 .7×) 101.88 ms (0 .7×)oneAPI (I1) 219.23 ms (0 .0×) 285.82 ms (0 .0×) 293.25 ms (0 .9×) 287.26 ms (0 .2×)

so NLPModels.jac coord! issues fifteen. These counts are read from the model and are exact. Dividing each measured GPU time by its kernel count gives a per-kernel cost. For the Hessian it is 11.0 μs, 10.8 μs, and 21.9 μs at the three ACOPF cases. For the Jacobian it is 10.0 μs, 10.6 μs, and 11.0 μs. The Jacobian per-kernel cost varies by at most 10% across those three cases, while the network grows by a factor of 665 in bus count. The Hessian per-kernel cost is flat between the two smaller cases and roughly doubles at the largest. The kernel count therefore accounts for the Jacobian cost across this range, and does not account for the Hessian cost at the largest case. That the fixed part is the kernel launch is an inference, supported by its magnitude and by the Jacobian, the cheaper kernel, coming out below the Hessian at every size. The floor is a property of the granularity of the abstraction, one kernel per pattern, and fusing the patterns of a model into a single generated kernel, or issuing them on concurrent streams, would lower it. The following section shows, however, that lowering it further carries little practical benefit, since NLP function evaluation is already a small share of a complete solve. 

## 8.5 Measuring the Benefits of GPU-Resident Callbacks 

Sections 8.3 and 8.4 measure callbacks in isolation. What a user experiences is a solve, in which the callbacks are one term among several. Speeding up the callbacks can therefore save only the share of the solve that the callbacks occupy. We run one interior-point method on two sets of instances and move only the device on which each of its two dominant costs is paid.

8.5.1 Solver Configurations and Reported Quantities 

Two of the four configurations pair a model on one device with a solver on the other. A user reaches for such a configuration when only one half of the solution procedure has been ported, and its cost is what the experiment is meant to expose. The bridge is WrapperNLPModel , provided by ExaModels.jl, which presents a model living on one device to a solver living on the other and forwards every callback through intermediate copy buffers. The solver is MadNLP.jl (Shin, Pacaud, and Montoison, 2025) with the LiftedKKT formulation (Pacaud, Shin, et al., 2024), which relaxes the equality constraints and condenses the KKT system so that the resulting matrix admits a sparse Cholesky-like factorization on a GPU. MadNLP.jl itself runs on either device, and the

Figure 8: Per-callback and composite speedup over single-threaded CPU (C3) on COPS instances, sorted by nnz J + nnz H . *Float32 precision. linear solver is chosen to match: LDLFactorizations.jl runs only on the CPU, and NVIDIA cuDSS only on the GPU. Each configuration is named by the device of the NLP solver and the device of the model. The four configurations are as close to one another as two devices allow. Each solves the same problem with the same formulation at the same tolerance, and each factorizes the same condensed KKT system with a limited-pivoting LDL factorization. LDLFactorizations.jl is chosen as the CPU linear solver because it has limited pivoting, similar to cuDSS, because it is purely single-threaded, and because it performs an LDL rather than an LBL factorization. The CPU and GPU configurations therefore run essentially the same algorithm, and the comparison isolates where the solver and the model run rather than which algorithm runs. A better CPU alternative exists, solving the augmented system with an LBL factorization with pivoting, but the algorithms are deliberately synchronized so that the experiment measures the effect of running the NLP solver and the NLP functions on the CPU against the GPU. The augmented route is preferred on the CPU because pivoting handles the indefinite KKT system stably without the conditioning penalty that condensation introduces; condensation is the price of a pivoting-free factorization, which the GPU requires. The Julia code that runs is nearly identical across the configurations. What differs is the array types it

The comparison is therefore between the same software on different hardware, together with the bridge between the two. Table 6 and Figure 10 separate the NLP function evaluation out of the solve, wherever that quantity can be measured without disturbing it. The evaluation column of Table 6 is the NLP function evaluation time contained in the solution time. In the fully GPU-resident configuration a callback only launches its kernels and nothing forces it to complete, so its evaluation time cannot be measured without altering the solve, and that entry is left blank. All four configurations were measured on platform N6, so the rows differ only in where the model and the NLP solver run. Iteration counts are reported alongside the times because they are not identical across configurations. The CPU and the GPU evaluate the same expressions in different orders and therefore round differently, so the iterate sequences diverge slightly.

Figure 10: Breakdown of the solution time across the four configurations of Table 6: Lukˇ san–Vlˇ cek Rosenbrock on the top row and PGLIB-OPF on the bottom, at three instances each. GPU-resident changes the case78484 epigrids solution time by a factor of 1.04. Moving the NLP solver to the GPU reduces the solution time from 147 s to 15.4 s, a factor of 9.6. Making the model GPU-resident reduces the solution time further, from 15.4 s to 7.48 s, a factor of 2.1. The overall reduction against the all-CPU configuration is a factor of 19.7. On the Rosenbrock instance, the two contributions are comparable: moving the NLP solver to the GPU gains a factor of 1.7, and making the model GPU-resident gains a factor of 1.7. The evaluation share of the solve behaves differently on the two devices. Figure 10 shows the breakdown across instance sizes, and the largest instances show the contrast most clearly. Each bar group is labeled by the device of the NLP solver over the device of the model evaluation, and each panel carries its own linear axis. The fully GPU-resident configuration ( G/G ) is drawn as a single segment, since its evaluation share is the quantity that cannot be measured without altering the solve. With the solver on the CPU, evaluation is a small part of the solve, 7% of the all-CPU configuration at case78484 epigrids . The factorization dominates so completely there that accelerating the callbacks could not repay the transfers and synchronization it would cost. Moving the solver to the GPU removes that dominance and leaves evaluation as the exposed term. 

57 Table 7: Julia compilation time for ExaModels.jl, varying the problem size and the number of distinct constraint kernels. Both sweeps ran on platform N6.                                            

> CPU GPU varying value model total model total
> Julia compilation, ExaModels.jl: problem size n
> 1000 318 4.7 s 16.2 s 67.0 s 10000 339 5.0 s 16.1 s 66.7 s 100000 324 4.8 s 16.3 s 66.9 s 1000000 371 5.0 s 16.1 s 66.2 s
> Julia compilation, ExaModels.jl: constraint kernels
> 1357 5.0 s 15.9 s 66.7 s 2504 5.2 s 16.2 s 67.7 s 4809 5.5 s 16.9 s 71.7 s 81.5 s 6.4 s 17.4 s 74.6 s 16 2.8 s 8.0 s 19.2 s 80.9 s

With the model still on the host, evaluation accounts for 52% of the same solve, and every callback pays for a transfer across the bus and for the completion barrier the transfer implies. Making the model GPU-resident removes both at once, which is why that configuration is the one where GPU acceleration of the callbacks actually pays. The configuration pairing a GPU-resident model with a solver on the CPU is the one that does not pay. The callbacks themselves do become faster, which the evaluation column of Table 6 shows. The solve does not follow, because the function evaluation is a small share of the total with a CPU solver, so cutting it changes the total by little. The achievable improvement is further limited by the bridge: each iteration must synchronize and transfer data between the host and the device, as every callback copies its arguments to the device and its results back. The practical reading is that the benefit of GPU-resident callbacks depends on the rest of the solution procedure already being on the device, and that porting the modeling layer alone is not a shortcut to it. 

## 8.6 Model Compilation Time 

The preceding subsections measure warm evaluation; this subsection measures the compilation cost paid before it. ExaModels.jl compiles specialized code separately for model creation and for each of the five NLP function callbacks of Section 8.2, one set of kernels per algebraic pattern. The reported time covers all of it: the model creation and the compilation of every kernel, not of a single one. Table 7 reports Julia’s own accounting of that time on the CPU and CUDA backends, during model construction and additionally through the first derivative evaluation and solve, varying the problem size at a fixed set of constraint kernels and the number of distinct constraint kernels at a fixed size. The constraint kernels are sixteen synthetic algebraic patterns over consecutive variable pairs, such as x2 

> i

+ xi+1 , sin( xi) xi+1 , exi−xi+1 , px2 

> i

+ 1 + xi+1 ,and tanh( xi) − xi+1 , each a distinct expression compiled as its own pattern. The kernel-count sweep splits the same total constraint count among the first k patterns, so the problem size stays fixed while only the number of distinct patterns varies. In the table, model is the compilation during model construction, total 

additionally includes the first derivative evaluation and the solve, and times are in milliseconds unless a unit is shown. On both backends the compilation time is flat in the problem size, within 0.9% on the GPU across a factor of 1 ,000, and grows with the number of distinct constraint kernels, by 7 .9× on the CPU and 1 .21 × on the GPU from 1 kernel to 16. The GPU increment is proportionally smaller because the measured time on the GPU includes compilation beyond the ExaModels.jl kernels, in CUDA.jl and its supporting compilation stack. That fixed cost, about 16 s, dominates the per-kernel cost, so the growth is masked; the per-kernel growth is present on both backends. Compilation is paid per algebraic pattern rather than per data point, so a model with few patterns compiles in seconds however large its data, and the cost scales only when the model gains structurally new constraints. 

58 The cost is also paid once per pattern set: recreating the same model, or creating it at a different size, triggers no further compilation. An ACOPF model compiled on a three-bus network can be recreated for the 78,484-bus network at no additional compilation cost, since every instance of the formulation shares the same algebraic patterns (Section 8.1). 

# 9 Conclusions 

We have presented ExaModels.jl, an AMS that exploits the partially separable, repetitive structure of large-scale NLPs to provide scalable NLP function evaluation and GPU acceleration. Two ideas underpin the system. The first is a SIMD abstraction that represents an NLP as a small number of algebraic patterns, each evaluated over many data points. It is realized through a parameterized expression tree that is fully encoded in the Julia type system. Because the problem structure is recognized at compile time, NLP function evaluation reduces to embarrassingly parallel loops over data points, so the same code runs natively on GPUs. The second is a coloring-free sparse AD algorithm . It performs the sparsity analysis on the parameterized expression tree itself, and assembles the Jacobian and Hessian directly into a partially compressed COO format. The parameterized expression tree is what makes the GPU acceleration possible. Each algebraic pattern compiles to a single kernel, so every NLP function callback becomes a data-parallel loop over the data points, with no device code in the model. Data-parallel execution on GPUs yields up to 76 × over single-threaded CPU evaluation, on the largest Lukˇ san–Vlˇ cek instances. NLP function evaluation also takes constant time. Once the device supplies enough threads, the cost is set by the number of algebraic patterns and not by the number of data points, so it does not grow as the problem grows. This is the O(1) behavior the SIMD abstraction was designed to deliver, and Section 8.3 demonstrates it. For the class of problems considered here, NLP function evaluation on a GPU is therefore effectively instantaneous, and what remains is the runtime efficiency of the optimization solver itself. The implications of this work are as follows. 

• For modelers and modeling library developers. GPU compatibility is the benefit worth pursuing, and either templatization or vectorization delivers it. A model that runs on the device makes a fully device-resident framework possible, and that is where the large computational gains are. The remaining differences are largely a matter of preference. Templatization keeps the constraint and objective syntax that mathematical programming languages have long used, and it can avoid the overhead of graph coloring. Vectorization integrates seamlessly with the surrounding machine learning workflows. 

• For solver developers. For classical applications, the major effort now belongs in reducing the number of factorizations the algorithm requires. Function evaluation is negligible beside them, especially on a GPU, since its cost does not grow with the number of data points. A solver can therefore assume in-place device-resident callbacks, exactly as it already assumes them on the CPU, and spend its effort on the linear algebra and on matching the reliability of CPU solvers. Progress there, in either reliability or efficiency, will make the results of this work more useful in practice.

# A Compiled LLVM Code for a Gradient Kernel 

This appendix reproduces the LLVM code that the Julia compiler generates for an ExaModels.jl gradient kernel. The listing supports the claim of Section 1.1: in a language built on multiple dispatch and JIT compilation, operator overloading can produce derivative evaluation code as free of overhead as source-code transformation. The model is the extended Rosenbrock objective (4), declared over a data iterator as 

@add obj(core, 100*(x[i-1]^2 - x[i])^2 + (x[i-1]-1)^2 for i in 2:N) . The function lowered is the per-data-point gradient kernel of Section 5.1. It receives one data point p, computes the gradient contribution of that point, and accumulates it into the output array. The kernel depends only on the argument types, not on the data iterator contents, so the same code serves every data point and every N . The listing was produced with @code llvm debuginfo=:none on Julia 1.12.6 with ExaModels.jl v0.11.2. The annotation comments, marked ;; , are ours, and everything else is reproduced verbatim. In the signature, the second argument is the parameterized expression tree, and its fully concrete type spells out the entire algebraic structure of the pattern (Section 4.2.3). In the body, the global indices of x[i-1] 

and x[i] are computed from the data point p and the offsets stored in the Var leaves. The body is a single basic block of straight-line code: the forward evaluation and the reverse pass are fully inlined, and there are no function calls, no branches, no dynamic dispatch, and no memory allocation. The generated code is also nearly minimal in its operation count. The body contains 13 floating-point operations, namely 8 multiplications, 3 subtractions, and 2 additions. The two sitofp instructions convert the integer constants embedded in the tree and involve no arithmetic on the data. For comparison, write 

u = xi−1, v = xi, a for the seed adjoint, and y for the gradient output. The same computation, both gradient 

64 contributions and their accumulation into y, can be hand-written with 12 operations: 

t = u · u, s = t − v, h = a · s, m = 200 h, p = u · m, p2 = p + p, r = u − 1, z = a · r, z2 = z + z, q = p2 + z2, yi−1 ← yi−1 + q, yi ← yi − m, 

that is, 5 multiplications, 3 subtractions, and 4 additions. We are not aware of a shorter sequence. The generated kernel is thus one operation off the best hand-written count we could construct. The difference is a factoring choice rather than interpretive overhead. The kernel forms the local derivatives 2 u, 2 s, and 2( u − 1) as multiplications during the forward pass, whereas the hand-written sequence replaces two of them with the doublings p + p and z + z. The listing shows the code shape that source-code transformation aims to produce, obtained here from the operator-overloading construction. The only code generation involved is the Julia compiler’s own lowering to the LLVM intermediate representation, which any compiled language performs. There is no separate source-level code-generation step.  

> 1

; Function Signature: gradient!(Array{Float64, 1}, ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(+)), ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(*)), Int64, ExaModels.Node1{typeof(Base.abs2), ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(-)), ExaModels.Node1{typeof(Base.abs2), ExaModels.Var{ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(+)), ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(-)), ExaModels.DataSource, Int64}, Int64}}}, ExaModels.Var{ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(+)), ExaModels.DataSource, Int64}}}}}, ExaModels.Node1{typeof(Base.abs2), ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(-)), ExaModels.Var{ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(+)), ExaModels.Node2{typeof(Base.:(-)), ExaModels.DataSource, Int64}, Int64}}, Int64}}}, Array{Float64, 1}, Array{Float64, 1}, Int64, Float64)

%10 = fmul double %9, 2.000000e+00  

> 31 32

;; load the constant coefficient 100 from the tree  

> 33

%"f::Node2.unbox" = load i64, ptr %"f::Node2", align 8  

> 34

%11 = sitofp i64 %"f::Node2.unbox" to double  

> 35 36

;; second term: locate and load x[i-1] and the constant 1; evaluate x[i-1] - 1  

> 37

%"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16" = getelementptr inbounds i8, ptr %"f::Node2", i64 32  

> 38

%"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.unbox" = load i64, ptr %"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16", align 8  

> 39

%12 = sub i64 %"p::Int64", %"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.unbox" 

65 40 %"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr" = getelementptr inbounds i8, ptr %"f::Node2", i64 40  

> 41

%"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr.unbox" = load i64, ptr %"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr", align 8  

> 42

%13 = add i64 %12, %"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr.unbox"  

> 43

%memoryref_offset23 = shl i64 %13, 3  

> 44

%14 = getelementptr i8, ptr %memoryref_data, i64 %memoryref_offset23  

> 45

%memoryref_data29 = getelementptr i8, ptr %14, i64 -8  

> 46

%15 = load double, ptr %memoryref_data29, align 8  

> 47

%"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr30" = getelementptr inbounds i8, ptr %"f::Node2", i64 48  

> 48

%"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr30.unbox" = load i64, ptr %"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr30", align 8  

> 49

%16 = sitofp i64 %"f::Node2.inner2_ptr16.inner2_ptr30.unbox" to double  

> 50

%17 = fsub double %15, %16  

> 51

%18 = fmul double %17, 2.000000e+00  

> 52 53

;; reverse pass: propagate the seed adjoint down the tree by the chain rule  

> 54

%19 = fmul double %11, %"adj::Float64"  

> 55

%20 = fmul double %10, %19  

> 56

%21 = fmul double %5, %20  

> 57 58

;; accumulate the first term’s contributions into y[i-1] and y[i]  

> 59

%memoryref_data32 = load ptr, ptr %"y::Array", align 8  

> 60

%22 = getelementptr i8, ptr %memoryref_data32, i64 %memoryref_offset  

> 61

%memoryref_data40 = getelementptr i8, ptr %22, i64 -8  

> 62

%23 = load double, ptr %memoryref_data40, align 8  

> 63

%24 = fadd double %21, %23  

> 64

store double %24, ptr %memoryref_data40, align 8  

> 65

%25 = getelementptr i8, ptr %memoryref_data32, i64 %memoryref_offset9  

> 66

%memoryref_data66 = getelementptr i8, ptr %25, i64 -8  

> 67

%26 = load double, ptr %memoryref_data66, align 8  

> 68

%27 = fsub double %26, %20  

> 69

store double %27, ptr %memoryref_data66, align 8  

> 70 71

;; accumulate the second term’s contribution into y[i-1]  

> 72

%28 = fmul double %18, %"adj::Float64"  

> 73

%29 = getelementptr i8, ptr %memoryref_data32, i64 %memoryref_offset23  

> 74

%memoryref_data92 = getelementptr i8, ptr %29, i64 -8  

> 75

%30 = load double, ptr %memoryref_data92, align 8  

> 76

%31 = fadd double %28, %30  

> 77

store double %31, ptr %memoryref_data92, align 8  

> 78

ret ptr %"y::Array"  

> 79

}

# B Per-Instance Benchmark Results 

This appendix presents the complete per-instance benchmark data. For each suite, we report the wall time in seconds for the callbacks NLPModels.obj , NLPModels.cons! , NLPModels.grad! , NLPModels.jac coord! ,and NLPModels.hess coord! . The backend column names the framework and, in parentheses, the platform of Table 2 on which it ran. An asterisk on a platform label marks timings taken in Float32 arithmetic. For each instance, the best time in each column is shown in bold.
